About The Town
SETTLED:
1718
INCORPORATED:
August 1, 1728
TYPE OF GOVERNMENT:
Town Meeting
LOCATION:
North Central Massachusetts, bordered by Townsend on
the north, Shirley on the east, Leominster and Lancaster
on the south, and Fitchburg and Ashby on the west. It is
about 47 miles from Boston and 29 miles from Worcester.
COUNTY:
Worcester
ELEVATION AT TOWN HALL:
Approximately 570 ft. above mean sea level
LAND AREA:
26.63 Square Miles
TOTAL AREA:
29 Square Miles
TOWN ROADS:
78.53 Miles
STATE ROADS:
6 Miles

8,375 (preliminary count)
DENSITY:
306 Persons Per Square Mile

Board of Selectmen
Board of Selectmen: Seated left to right - Ann P. Hall, Chairman August N. Misner and James T. Van
Buren. Standing left to right - Executive Secretary Richard J. Eley and Town Counsel Timothy S. Hillman.
At the conclusion of 1981 and with it, six months of budgetary limitations due to Proposition 2½, it has again
been demonstrated that we are extremely fortunate in Lunenburg to have responsible and dedicated Department Heads
who have a close working relationship with the personnel in their respective departments, and who together have
carried out their duties in a very commendable manner. This harmony and "can do" attitude is not always found in
communities our size, and was quite evident this year. It must also be pointedout that through the collective efforts
of the Selectmen, Assessors, Treasurer, Tax Collector, and other elected officials, and the dedication of personnel
on various Boards and Committees, especially the Finance Committee, the mandate of the voters was carriedout with
a minimum of disruption and/or curtailment of services to our residents.
In May, the Board amended the Traffic Rules and Orders of the Town to enforce "No Parking Zones" during
certain times on Lakefront Road in order to effect better control and minimize the problems in that area, Also, the
system of having rubbish containers on the Lakefront was continued, and although this program was revised with re-
spect to only certain months of the year due to budgetary limitations, it has proven to be a program well received.
In August, the Study Commission reported to this Board that Lunenburg should continue to receive services from
the Nashoba Associated Boardsof Health based upon the fact that it would not be possible to acquire all the services
now received at a more reasonable price. The Study Commission also reported that communications and misunder-
standings were major problems with regard to Nashoba. The Selectmen and Nashoba, cognizant of this fact, have
undertaken steps to eliminate misunderstandings and to improve communications. A detailed report of Nashoba act-
ivities during 1981 may be found in the Board of Health report.
In November, after several years of study, research, evaluation, and many hours of meetings, etc., the new
Sanitary Landfill located off Youngs Road was officially opened and operational, and should meet the needs of the
Town for several years to come.
- 4 -
===PAGE 7===
The Board of Selectmen, acting in its capacity as
the Licensing Authority, reviewed and revised the fees
of various licenses and permits under its jurisdiction.
The majority of the new fees will go into effecton Jan-
uary 1, 1982 and will generate several thousands of
dollars to the Town.
This report would not be complete without call--
ing attention to the resignation of Attorney Robert J.
Larkin on July 1st after serving our community for over
thirty-five years as Town Counsel. His resignation was
accepted by this Board with the deepest of regret, and
his years of dedicated service to our Town will always
be remembered. The docket will forever show his ac-
complishments on the Town's behalf. Also on July 1st,
the Board was pleased to confirm the appointment. of
Attorney Timothy S. Hillman as our new Town Counsel,
and we all wish him well in this, a most vital position.
In conclusion, the Board of Selectmen wishes to
express its appreciation to our residents for their con-
tinued support of our mutual goals for the Town of Lun-
enburg.

Board of Health
The following report was received by this Board from J. David Naparstek, Director of Public Health, of the
Nashoba Associated Boards of Health which consists of sixteen member towns.
Boards of Health are among the oldest town boards in Massachusetts. Your Board of Health is responsible for
the preservation and promotion of the health of the Town's residents. Certain police powershave been delegated to
them by the Commonwealth, such as the enforcement of the Sanitary and Environmental Codes and the control of
communicable diseases. Other responsibilities relate to promoting health in the community in such areas as clinics,
dental health and health education.
The Nashoba Associated Boards of Health helps your Board of Health carry out both the mandated and the re-
commended public health activities. Your Board in association with other member Boards, decides what services
Nashoba will offer; Nashoba's professional staff provides these services. The following services were provided by
the Nashoba Associated Boards of Health in 1981:
COMMUNITY HEALTH SERVICE
Well Child Conferences - These clinics are intended to promote optimum health among pre-school children
and to prevent the spread of communicable disease through immunizations. Unfortunately, low utilization in a num-
ber of towns and budget cutbacks necessitated the elimination of these clinics on July 1, 1981.

Lunenburg

Well Oldster and Adult Clinics - This is another health promotion program designed to prevent disease and
disability through early detection. These clinics are offered on a regular basis to provide continuity of care.

NASHOBA NURSING SERVICE The Nursing Service provides both mandated and recommended services. Some
examples of the mandated activities performed by Nashobanurses include communicable disease investigation, tuber-
culosis control, and premature infant assistance. The Nursing Service also serves as a certified home health agency
(i.e., visiting nurse association) for 15 towns. The Nursing Service is developing an industrial health program which
began in 1981.

MEDICAL-SOCIAL WORK CONSULTANT - Nashoba's medical-social consultant is available to assist residents cope
with medical, social and emotional problems. Such a service is not a mandated one, yet it is one that is provided
by a caring community to its citizens. The medical-social consultant serves patients through direct counseling, in-
formation and referral, consultation with health agencies and professionals, and support groups.

DENTAL HEALTH SERVICES 104 The Massachusetts Department of Public Health strongly recommends that each Board
of Health provide or sponsor dental health services for school children. Nashoba does both. Our school-based den-
tal health program has been serving students since 1953. This program provides an oral health education program for
all students in Grades Kindergarten to 8; screening for Grades Kindergarten and 3; and topical fluoride for Grades
2, 5 and 8. Nashoba introduced a new program in 1981, a school-based fluoride mouthrinse program. Students in
Grades 1 - 6 are given the opportunity to rinse with a fluoride solution once a week. This program is funded by the
State Department of Public Health. In 1981, Ashburnham, Ashby, Berlin, Lancaster and Townsend participated in
the program. All Nashoba towns are eligible to participate.

ENVIRONMENTAL HEALTH SERVICES - Most of the mandated responsibilities of the local Board of Health are car-
ried out by Nashoba's Environmental Health Division. Under the State Sanitary Code, Nashoba inspects housing,
recreation camps, farm labor camps and restaurants. The State Environmental Code requires regulation of sewage
disposal systems, bathing beaches, swimming pools and family campgrounds. There are numerous other State laws
that are enforced by Nashoba. In addition, Nashoba provides laboratory services for testing drinking and bathing
waters.

ADMINISTRATION - The challenge of the coming years will be to carry out all of the activities that are mandated
by law and also continue to provide the optional, but extremely important, public health services in atime of reduced
funding. The reduction of State personnel places an additional burden on local agencies. Many of the State Codes
which are locally enforced are being rewritten by the State to be stronger and more comprehensive. Each Board of
Health is legally and morally bound to enforce these regulations to protect the public's health.

Planning Board
The Planning Board has been active
again this year, both reacting to problems
that arise, and planning for problems that
are expected to arise in the future.
Lunenburg's Master Plan, originally
adopted in 1961, has not been updated since
that point. The Board feels that it is imper-
ative to complete a new Master Plan now.
There are several concerns that cannot be
addressed in the day to day administration of
the Town, but rather requires an in depth
study. Some of the more important concerns
include the impact of the completion of I-
190, Pyramid Mall, and Digital; water qual-
ity and resource protection; as well as an
attempt to protect the current rural quality
of the Town without unduly restricting de-
velopment.
In attempting to prevent the increased
costs that in the past the Town has had to
absorb due to developments, the Board's Sub-
division Regulations were amended to allow
the Board to consider the adequacy of access
of public ways leading to proposed subdiv-
isions. The Board is empowered to require
that the subdivider improve such public way
in order to provide the adequate access.
The Regulations were also amended to
increase pavement width for minor streets to
20 feet, delete the requirement for granite
catch basin inlets, change the curbing re-
Planning Board: Standing left to right - Chairman Robert J. Ebersole,
quirement to a "cape cod" type, and various
Patricia C. Lewis and James M. Sudolnik. Not present when
administrative changes. These changes were
photo was taken were Charles H. B. Arning and James A. Mann.
adopted after working with subdivisions in
Town, one of which is Canterbury Drive, a 10 lot subdivision off Arbor Street. Major construction on this street
was started in 1981.
A preliminary subdivision plan was presented for a 34 lot subdivision at the end of Island Road. The plan was
reviewed, found to be in accordance with the Board's Regulations, and approved. The applicant has not yet sub-
mitted a definitive plan.
The Annual Town Meeting required the Planning Board to report on several Articles. As aresult of the Board's
subcommittee report on private road acceptances, the Hickory Hills Landowners, Inc. submitted to the Town Meeting
for acceptance: Island Road, Peninsula Drive and Peninsula Drive Extension. The Planning Board had previously
reviewed the ways, found them to be in compliance with the above report's standards, and recommended approval.
The Town voted to accept these roads. The Town voted to rezone the Page and Gleason property on Pleasant Street
from residential to commercial. The Board reviewed the proposal and as the property was currently used commercially,
it was felt to be a proper action for the Town to take. Professional buildings were added to uses permissible in a
residential zone on special authorization by the Board of Appeals. The Board determined that the Board of Appeals
would provide sufficient safeguard to assure proper placement of such buildings.
- 9 -
===PAGE 12===
In order to provide protection to the public, establish a uniform practice in the design and layout of driveways,
and to provide for adequate drainage, the Board developed standards for locating driveways and entrances to prop-
erties in Town. A public hearing was held where major concerns were aired. The Board revised the standards to re-
flect these concerns and presented the amendment to the Town Meeting, which voted approval.
In response to Proposition 2½, the Planning Board voted to establish fees for submission of zoning amendments
for hearing costs ($35), and for submission of plans not requiring subdivision approval ($10). This should help the
Town to recoup some of the processing costs involved. The Board has been involved in the Environmental Impact
Review process for the proposed Pyramid Mall near Fort Pond in Lancaster. This new development is expected to
increase traffic in Lunenburg, increase development in Town, while on the positive side may increase employment
for Town residents.
Mr. James Mann, as the Planning Board's representative, has worked with the Selectmen and the Federal In-
surance Administration to determine those areas in Town subject to flooding. This mapping andzoning for such areas
is required under the Federal Flood Insurance Acts and action must be taken at the 1982 Annual Town Meeting.
Chairman Ebersole attended a conference of the Massachusetts Federation of Planning Boards held in November.
The Planning Board accepted with regret the resignation of David P. Demers due to educational pursuits and
the establishment of his own consulting firm. The Board will miss his expertise. This vacancy was filled by James M.
Sudolnik, who was appointed by a unanimous joint vote of the Planning Board and Board of Selectmen to fill out the
balance of Mr. Demer's unexpired term.
The Planning Board wishes to express its appreciation for the cooperation other Town Boards have extended,
including increased communication on vital Town matters.
Board of Assessors
Board of Assessors - left to right - Secretary Jacqueline A. Keeler,
William F. Reed, Bernard J. Paton and Chairman Kathryn M. Herrick.
The year 1981 was a very busy year for the Assessors, with the "Sudbury Decision", Classification and Prop-
osition 2, it became more important than ever to bring the properties in Town to 100% Full and Fair Market Value.
The decision of how best to accomplish this was put to the voters in a special warrant just prior to the Annual Town
Meeting in April 1981. As may have been expected, the voters said NO to a Revaluation. However, given the al-
ternative of having the State hire a firm and charge the cost back to the Town via the next year's Cherry Sheet re-
turns, an Article to hire an outside firm was passed.
- 10 -
===PAGE 13===
The Assessors drew up specifications based on State guidelines and solicited bids. OF the threefirms who sub-
mitted bids, the firm of Robert J. Finnegan, though not the lowest bidder, was the unanimous choice of the Board.
The Board felt that theirpublic information policy and their maintenance program would best serve the Town's needs.
A contract was signed on June 15, 1981. Since that date, the firm has hired and trained a crew of Data Collectors
and the Revaluation is in full swing. Once the information is gathered, it will be converted to machine readable
data and field reviewed. When this is completed, taxpayers will receive notification of the new value placed on
their property, at which time they may set up informal hearings to question their assessments if they so desire.
Since all assessments are currently a fraction of full value, all will rise when the Revaluation is completed.
It should be noted, however, that the tax rate will decrease in the same proportion as the increase in valuation. The
largest increase is expected to be in vacant land. Historically, land values in Lunenburg have been low. Because
property is to be assessed at "Full and Fair Market Value", the main criterion for determining this value is the sales
history over the last three years. However, there is a glimmer of hope for those taxpayers with parcels of land of
five acres ormore. A new section hasbeen added to the General Laws Chapter 61B Recreational Land which per-
mits lower assessments for parcels of at least five acres of vacant unimproved land. A more detailed and complete
list of exemptions is given at the end of this report.
As a result of implementation of Proposition 2½ and the necessary budget cuts, the tax rate was reduced from
$54.00 for Fiscal Year 1981 to $44.00 per thousand valuation for Fiscal Year 1982. What constraints will be re-
quired this year will depend in part upon the results of the Revaluation, i.e. how much additional valuation results
from the process. We expect preliminary figures in time for the Annual Town Meeting in April 1982.
The Board of Assessors wishes to remind taxpayers of certain exemptions that have been enacted concerning the
value of property, both residential and non-residential. Some of these exemptions are subject to qualifications of
the land itself, while others are dependent upon qualifications of the owners. These exemptions may not exceed
specific amounts. If a taxpayer feels that he qualifies for any of the following exemptions, he should contact the:
Assessors' Office and request an application which lists all the requirements that must be met.
Section 17
- Widows, minors whose fathers are deceased, and persons over 70- $2,000 in valuation or atax
of $175, whichever is greater.
Section 22
- Disabledor wounded veterans, or their wives, widows or parents - $2,000 to $10,000 valuation
or a tax from $175 to $875, whichever is greater depending on degree of disability.
Section 22E
- Veterans with yearly certificate from Veterans' Administration indicating 100% disability.
Section 37
- Blind persons - $5,000 valuation or a tax of $437.50, whichever is greater.
Section 41
- Persons aged 70 or over - $4,000 valuation or a tax of $500, whichever is greater.
Section 41A
- Tax deferral for certain persons 65 years of age or over with receipts of under $20,000 an-
nually to place the taxes on his domicile and one acre of land on a deferred payment plan, the
taxes to be paid upon settlement of the estate plus 8% interest.
Section 42
- Unmarried widows of police officers or firefighters killed in the line of duty - $8,000 valuation
or a tax of $700, whichever is greater.
Section 43
- Minor orphans of police officers or firefighters killed in the line of duty -$8,000 valuation or a
tax of $700, whichever is greater.
Clause 61A
Section 9
- Provides for assessment of agricultural or horticultural land at value based upon its agricultural
or horticultural use.
Clause 61
Section 1-6
- Provides for assessment of forest land managed under a planned program and certification of a
State Forester.
Chapter 61B
Recreational Land (1) Provides for assessment of landnot less than five acres if it is retained in substantially a nat-
ural, wildor open condition or in a landscaped condition in such a manner as to allow to a sig-
nificant extent the preservation of wildlife and other natural resources. (2) Land deemed to be
recreational land which is devoted primarily to recreational use and is available to the general
public or members of a non-profit organization.

Tree Warden
Again this year caterpi crawled rampant over
trees and shrubs, eating their way over acres of trees,
and in doing so, reduced the amount of oxygen released
into the atmosphere that is normally given off by leaves
of trees.
During Fiscal 1981, 232 diseased trees were cut,
chipped and burned. The Department of Natural Re-
sources gave 5 days ($700.00) of tree removal work and
two days of tagging trees suspected of having Dutch Elm
disease. Samples were sent to the University of Mass-
achusetts for analysis. The Town was reimbursed $582.99
by the County. A total of $11,041.90 was spent under
the Dutch Elm Account.
Work accomplished under the supervision of the
Tree Warden amounted to $8,208.42. The Chipping
Program was continued whereby townspeople could bring
brush to be chipped to the Town Highway Garage on the
second and fourth Saturdays of the month for as long as
money remained in the account for this purpose. Towns-
Richard W. Brown, Tree Warden
people seem to appreciate this service.

Park Commission
FULFUR
Park Commission: left to right - Chairman Richard A. Kinney and Park Superintendent Gregory
Bingham. Not present when photo was taken were Richard J. Bishop and Armand E. St. Arnaud.
The Summer Recreational Program again enjoyed a successful year. Richard Stachowicz provided the leader-
ship for our fine staff which consisted of Ann O'Sullivan at the Wallis Park Playground, Brenda St. Arnaud and Jane
Gallagher at the Center Playground, and Beth Arciprete for the Arts and Crafts Program.
The High School Basketball League consisted of eight teams. Oakmont and Fitchburg won their respective
divisions. Fitchburg won the League Championship by defeating Oakmont by twenty points. The Junior League
Basketball Program had another good year under the direction of Jay Long. Attendance was strong throughout the
entire program. This year saw a greater number of girls participating. Jay is to be commended for making this pro-
gram as successful as it was.
The Men's Softball Leaguel had another good year. The R. A. Powell team won both the divisional and playoff
championships. Seven teams were involved which was a drop of two teams from the previous year. Our Commission
would like to thank all those involved in our programs. Their dedication and hard work resulted in a very successful
year.
The Life Course which was installed at Marshall Park in 1980 received a lot of use during 1981. Again, we
express our thanks to all those responsible for the funding and personal time which made this course a reality. Our
thanks go to the Memorial Fund for the renovation of the Town Bandstand. The cost of the repairs came from the
funds given in memory of Sherwood G. Winchester. Many more years of summer concerts will now be possible.
Once again our special thanks go out to our Town Clerk, Mary Padula, and her fine staff. This year we wel-
comed Greg Bingham to our staff as our new Park Superintendent. His dedication and hard work has once again made
our Park facilities the envy of surrounding communities. Our job as Commissioners has been a lot easier since Greg
joined our staff. Thanks Greg, and many years of success ahead,
The first year of Proposition 2½ has been a challenge. The Commission had some hard decisions to make before
arriving at the programs to be cut back. Were it not for the many new volunteers, the impact of Proposition 2½ would
have been more severe. Our special thanks go out to all of them.

Housing Authority
Housing Authority - left to right - Executive Secretary Eileen M. Alleva,
Ella Rae Denault, Richard I. White and Josephine C. Meloon.
The Lunenburg Housing Authority is in its twelfth year in the operation of the facilities at 131 White Street
known as the Pearl Brook Housing for the Elderly.
Presently, we have forty-four apartments occupied by single persons and four occupied by couples. The ten-
ants have organized the Pearl Brook Tenants' Association which meets on a regular basis and arranges interesting pro-
jects for the benefit and enjoyment of its members. Mrs. Olive Goodnow holds the office of President and is the
principal liaison between the tenants and the Authority members,
The tenants are provided with weekly transportation. The shut-ins are provided with "Meals on Wheels", and
the Nashoba Associated Boards of Health provide the Well Oldster Clinic monthly for the tenants. Home Health
Care provides household duties and shopping.
The requirements for occupancy are that the applicants be at least 65 yearsof age and in need of good housing.
Rents are variable in that no more than 25% of income can be charged for rent which includes heat, water and elec-
tricity. The rising cost of energy makes it very difficult for the Authority to remain within its budget.
We invite anyone who is interested in our facilities to inquire at the Housing Authority Office where Mrs.
Eileen Alleva, Executive Secretary, will be available to answer questions.

===PAGE 18===
Library Trustees
Library Trustees - Seated left to right - June Dettenrieder and Barbara T. Murray. Standing
left to right - Sheila A. Jalutkewicz, Edward A. O'Brien, Director Elizabeth H. Burnap,
and C. David Hobson. Not present when photo wastaken was Chairman Patrick J. Slattery.
Libraries in Massachusett are finding diverse ways to with budget cuts since the passage of Proposition 2½.
Although no public library charges for the most basic services--issuing cards, checking books out and in, and reference
work many have increased fines and now charge substantial amounts to reserve books and replace cards. Some
charge for use of meetings rooms that had been free. Contrary to all past library practices, some must now devise
ways to reduce the demand for services. While Ritter Memorial Library has not yet resorted to many new fees, the
fine charged for overdue books has been raised from 2¢ to 5¢ per day, resulting in a substantial increase in the amount
turned over to the Town. A positive side effect has been aslight drop in the number of late returns. It is hoped that
it will not become necessary to charge other new fees because in each such instance, the library becomes less "pub-
lic", less "free" and less available to at least some Townspeople.
The most noticeable change brought about by budget restrictions at Ritter Memorial Library has been there-
duction in hours open. Since July 1, 1981, the library has been open 48 hours per week, 7 hours less per week than
before. The hours cut from the schedule were the least busy hours of the week, and, being late in the day, allowed
for some reduction in the amount of electricity used, as well as a small saving of heat. This relatively minor change
in hours open does not seem to have seriously inconvenienced patrons, although Townspeople in Lunenburg have been
accustomed to longer library hours than are residents in most towns of the same size. Seven community groups, in
addition to the Friends of the Library and the Trustees, have used the library meeting room during the year, but shorter
evening hours have prevented some groups from meeting there this fall.
An unusual number of changes in library personnel occurred during the year. Diane Polk, who had become
Director in March 1980, left in May 1981 to become Consultant for Children's Services in the Central Massachusetts
Regional Library System. Elizabeth Burnap, Assistant Director since December 1974, was appointed Director. Since
it was necessary to cut staff hours in compliance with budget restrictions, the Assistant Director's position was reduced
to half-time, and has been filled since July 1981 by Elizabeth Vezina, who has a Master's Degree in Library Science
from Simmons. Richard Harper, Custodian for many years, retired at the end of June, and two of three Pages left
during the year, resulting in three more positions filled by new employees. Library service continued without inter-
ruption despite all these changes due to the dependable support of the three remaining staff members: Emily Foster,
Children's Librarian; Nancy Bigelow, Library Technician; and Kelly Horgan, Page.
- 16 -
===PAGE 19===
Because the Assistant Director's position had, been reduced to half-time and the other new personnel were at
starting rate rather than the higher rates earned by the former employees, it was not necessary to cut hours of the re-
maining staff and a substantial amount of money already voted into the budget for personnel could be allocated for
other purposes. The total for books and other library materials was restored to slightly below the FY81 level. This
is still a serious cut, considering the rate of inflation, but it has not reduced book purchasing power to an impossible
level. Other budget categories--postage, telephone, electricity, heat, and equipment are also at levels comparable
to FY81, and, with care, should be sufficient for the rest of this fiscal year, barring an exceptionally cold winter.
Also, $600 less than last year is available for building repairs and maintenance; and this is a real cause for concern
because for many years the library has been restricted to doing only the most urgent repairs and minimal maintenance.
It will be remarkable if this category does not have to be overspent in FY82.
Children's Services, under the direction of Emily Foster, experienced another year of continuous activity and
growth. A total of 128 children participated in the summer reading program, "Destination Otherwhere", reading
2, 210 books. Special events in conjunction with this program were: a magic show, a treasure hunt, T-shirt designing,
a chalk-drawing contest, a wild animal talk, dancing on the green, a puppet show and movies. An additional reading
program was started in the fall. Three pre-school story hours with capacity enrollments helped three to five year olds
learn to enjoy the library, as did a new program, "Movies for the Very Young", using regional films. Other children's
activities included craft workshops and after school and vacation movies. The Primary School classes visited regu-
larly throughout the school year.
The Friends of the Library have maintained their vital supportive role again this year, making possible many
things that could not have been done without their volunteer time and financial backing. Funds raised with their
annual yard sale and "Book and Bake" sale, two roller skating parties, the boutique table, and sale of their cookbook,
"Favorites of the Friends", have been used to purchase all craft and food supplies for the children's programs and the
books awarded at the end of the reading program. The Friends provided the means to pay all the performers for
children's programs and sponsored the annual Children's Art Festival. A very substantial recent gift from the Friends
is a new paperback rack, allowing a much more attractive display of adult paperback books, Friday evening "Inter-
national Nights" arranged by the Friends this year included talks with slides on England, Ireland, Labrador, the
Pacific Northwest, and a Japanese tea ceremony. The morning "Interest Series" presented programs on medicinal
herbs, spring decorating, art prints, egg decorating and makeup techniques. Again this year, the Friends purchased
a pass for Children's Museum in Boston which admits a family free and can be reserved through the library. The fol-
lowing gifts have enriched the library's collection and are much appreciated: two books on Cape Cod given by the
Women's Club; "Shallow Waters", given by the Robert Moisan family in memory of Vern Ketonen; "The Story of
Writing", given by John Boursy, and a year's subscription to "New England Farm Journal", given by Robert Stockwell.
Many generous donations of used books were made, some of which were added to the collection, and the rest given
to the Friends for their sales.
In spite of shorter hours and reduction in staff, the library has handled a larger circulation of materials than
ever before. A total of 52, 193 items were borrowed as follows: 25, 595 from the Adult collection, 24, 619 from the
Children's collection, and 1, 979 from the Young Adult section. This represents a 6% increase in materials circulated.
The challenge underlying all library service during 1981 has been to cope with change, and some of this change has
been of a nature not experienced before by the present Library Trustees and staff. An increase in book circulation
has always been one way for a library to judge that "we must be doing something right" -- tangible evidence that
the desired books are being bought, that Townspeople are satisfied with programs and services, and that growth is
taking place. But increased circulation and growing number of people in the building to study or participate in
programs must now be handled in fewer hours by a smaller staff working with a reduced budget, compelling all con-
cerned to realize that growth in services may no longer be arealistic goal. Some of the less public aspects of library
operation are already being neglected and could eventually accumulate until all service is adversely affected.
While larger libraries and regional systems are now looking to automation for the answer to the problem of
shrinking budgets, this is not feasible at the present time for a town the size of Lunenburg. It is encouraging that
Ritter Memorial Library has not yet been restricted financially to the point that services, other than the hours already
cut, must be deliberately curtailed. Moreover, Townspeople can be confident that all funds allocated for the library
are carefully managed to provide maximum return. The record of years past indicate that Lunenburg values its library
to the extent that financial support will continue at a functional level.

Highway Department
If a motorist should skid on wet pavement or a
patch of ice, have a blowout, or just take his eyes
off the road for a second, he could be among the 300
to 750 people killed each year in accidents involving
rural mailboxes. Accident records from one state
indicate that in a single year it had 3,326 accidents
involving rural ilboxes, resulting in 15 fatalities,
725 personal injuries, and 2,585 property damage
accidents. Those who are not killed are often blinded
and disfigured for life because the boxes and their
supports penetrate the windshield and hit the victim
in the face.
Ibox owners are limitedonly by their imag-
ination. Steel tractor wheels, old-time stoves, water
pumps, plow shares, milk cans filled with concrete
and massive I-beams are but a few of the devices
used to support mailboxes. Although such supports
may be artistic to some, most are serious roadside
hazards to motorists.
Design specifications for rural mailboxes are
promulgated by the U.S. Postal Service, but spec-
ifications for mailbox supports are not. In fact, with
the exception of one state, there appears to be no
national, state or local standards for the size, shape
or number of rural mailbox supports.
Mailboxes are hazardous primarily because of
their mounting height. Most boxes are mounted ap-
proximately 42 inches above ground to facilitate the
George G. Munyon, Jr., Highway Superintendent
placement of mail in the box by the carrier. This
height places the boxes in direct with the wind-
shield of many vehicles. Mounting of several boxes on a wood board, a common rural occurrence, compounds the
problem since the board can spear through the windshield.
Ideally, a single support post should have sufficient tensile resistance and friction in the ground to hold the box
until it is propelled forward at the speed of the vehicle, then pulled from the ground and ridden over or thrown off.
One state has tentatively chosen the 2 lb./ft. steel U-post as the most cost effective of the single supports it tested
and intends to implement its use. Standard 2 by 2 by 4 inch mailbox mounting bracket angles, one on each side,
are recommended for attaching boxes to both the single and multiple standards. The box is attached to the angle
with 3/16 inch stove bolts. The angles are attached to the standards with a 3/8 inch bolt, lock washer and nut.
(The above are excerpts from Public Works Magazine - November 1981.)
The following is a report of services performed and projects accomplishedon the Town's approximately 80 miles
of accepted roads:
Labor - For the second year in a row, we had less snow than normal and subsequently less snow overtime, resulting
in our turning back $13,959.26 to the Town.
Chapter 90 Improvement - A mix-in-place surface was applied to Summer Street and Electric Avenue, and guardrail
was purchased, at a total cost of $61,333.80.

Northfield, Memorial Drive, Lakeview, Laurel Lane, Pine Grove and Rennie Streets. Also, 655.64 tons of patch
was used at a cost of $15,059.67. Catch basins were cleaned, drainage work, brush cutting and chipping, roadside
mowing, sweeping, purchase of guardrail and small tools also come under this account. Total cost was $128,390.42.
Snow Removal - The purchase of salt, sand, snow fence, calcium chloride and hired equipment cost $52,722.66.

materials pertinent to the upkeep of all equipment, plus gas, oil, grease, diesel fuel, acetylene, oxygen, plow and
grader condition. blades, chains and small tools. A total of $71,306.04 was spent to maintain the Town's equipment in top

equipment and special building maintenance cost $9,828.40.

Police Department
Attempting to comply with the mandates of
Proposition 2½, and still provide adequate police
coverage for the community, was the challenge that
had to be met at the start of 1981.
Fortunately, changes in funding allowed the
continuing of the operation of three cruisers, and
we were able to maintain basic coverage on all
shifts. To compensate for the elimination of addi-
tional policing on Whalom Lakefront, parking was
restricted between the hours of 9PM and 6AM. This
measure helped control loitering with some 500
parking tickets being issued during the latter part
of 1981, and approximately $3,000.00 in fines are
due to the Town. New State legislation is designed
to enable communities to collect their own park-
ing fines. This should result in a more prompt re-
turn of these funds, as non-compliance will cause
loss of registration and/or license.
During the past year, of the 262 motor ve-
hicle accidents, four resulted in fatalities with two
of these occurring on Route 2A. Four armed rob-
beries, 19 breaking and entering into private homes
and businesses, 147 larcenies and 24 reported sto-
len vehicles amounted to $237,621.00 worth of
Frederick B. Hobbs, Chief of Police
stolen property of which $56,386.00 was recovered.
- 20 -
===PAGE 23===
The Department had under investigation 42 hit and run accidents, 42 assaults, plus 12 assaults on police of-
ficers, 74 acts of vandalism, 18 disturbances, 8 acts of trespassing and reports of 13 missing or runaway persons. The
police also responded to 609 alarms, false or otherwise, conducted 509 house checks, took 29 persons into "protec-
tive custody" and received 7, 216 miscellaneous calls for service.
During the year all regular and reserve officers received First Aid and CPR training to keep them in compli-
ance with the First Responder Law. Patrolman John A. Busack resigned in July to accept a position with the United
States Postal Service, and Patrolman John E. Baker is still convalescing after being struck by a motor vehicle on
October 1, 1979.

Historical Commission
The Historical Commission is currently taking an inventory of the Town. This inventory is the first step and the
essential step in preservation. It provides a record of the areas, buildings, monuments, sites, burial grounds and
structures which are of historical, architectural, or archeological significance to the community.
The basis of all local preservation planning is this inventory. This would provide the local Planning Board,
traffic committee, Conservation Commission and other municipal agencies with information about historic properties
which should be considered in community planning. The inventory provides the information with which the Mass-
achusetts Historical Commission can help to defend historic properties from adverse effect caused by publicly funded
and licensed projects such as, highway construction, urban renewal and subsidized housing.
This inventory will provide the basis by which the local Historical Commission, or other local group conducting
an inventory, can determine the priorities for suggesting properties to the Massachusetts Historical Commission for
consideration. The inventory also becomes part of the State Historic Preservation Plan and is considered in all State
planning.
Lunenburg's Historical Commission welcomes and encourages local citizens to participate in this endeavor.
All those wishing to volunteer their services may contact a member of this Commission. Our meetings are posted at
the Town Hall and are open to the public. The Historical Commission comprises the following membership: Co-
Chairmen, Gwendolyn K. Holmes and Martha J. Moore, Robert J. Ebersole, Paul B. Porter, Raymond J. Powell, Jr.
and Glenn S. Stillman.
Veterans' Services
This Department has complied with the Proposition
2½ cutbacks and reports that due to various "outlets". it has
had the most expense-free year in the past 13 years. This
is due to the fact that recipients must sign a release form
stating that they will repay the Town on a voluntary basis
from any monies they have accrued fromother sources, such
as unemployment compensation, sick benefits, pensions,
etc. This has caused many persons to reconsider applying
for benefits.
As the years go by, the World War 2 Veteran is ap-
proaching 65 years of age and has the Veterans' Adminis-
tration to fall back on. The Korean and Vietnam Veteran
has utilized the new program "Veterans Outreach" and has
become self-sufficient and educated as to his/her benefits.
The caseload of this Department has shifted immense-
ly from past years and this has resulted in actually saving
monies for the Town. However, this could change over-
night. There seems to be a cycle -- years of plenty and
then years of need. The year 1981 could be considered a
year of plenty, but the Veterans' Agent is uncertain as to
what the future holds, and has suggested a cut inhis budget
Alfred H. Gauthier, Veterans' Agent
and to revert back to the Reserve Fund if need be.
The Veterans' Agent wishes to point out that an attempt is still being made to combine the Veterans' Depart-
ment with the Welfare Department. The Veterans' Agent wishes to take this opportunity to thank all Town Officials,
the Town Clerk and her wonderful staff who have assisted him in the past, and hopefully will continue to do so in the
future.

===PAGE 25===
Personnel Committee
From the Personnel Committee's point of view, the past year might better be described as "reflective" than
"active". Probably due to the constraints of Proposition 2½, there have been virtually no proposals for departmental
expansion or revision brought before the Committee. New hires have been limited to replacements in staff depleted
by terminations. Further, the Committee has undertaken some major revisions in the Salary Administration Plan over
the past several years. This year offered an opportunity to watch how well they work. By and large, the results
have been satisfying, thanks in large measure to the department heads responsible for administering the provisions
of the "Plan". It is not, after all, the rules which govern, rather the rules serve as guidelines for government, and
work no better than the ability of administrators to use them.
The Committeeha taken advantage of this "lull" to do some careful review of what is going on in the business
world, as it relates to employer/employee relations. Sooner or later, many of the changes will affect our munici-
pality, and we will need policies and/or bylaw provisions tailored to dealing with them. Municipalities are not
always subject to every change that takes place in the management/labor scene in industry, but it is necessary to be
aware of such changes and to constantly reassess the Town's position in relation to them. The point of all of this is
to minimize our vulnerability to problems which in the past our bylaw has treated sketchily, or not at all. Thus,
the Committee has put considerable time into examination of such matters as adequacy of pre-employment informa-
tion gathering, termination procedures and the like. This study is ongoing, and will eventually result in some changes,
either in the bylaw itself or in the practical procedures recommended to department heads administering the "Plan".
The Salary Schedule proposed at this year's Town Meeting will containonly one new category - that of "High-
way Supervisor/Landfill Operations Manager". This does not create any new position or additional salary, but merely
brings the stipend being paid the Highway Supervisor for Landfil management under the Salary Schedule, as prescribed
by the "Plan"; something which was not possible until the operation was underway and the responsibilities involved
could be properly assessed. Aside from this, no revisions are proposed except for what the Committee feels is a
moderate cost-of-living increase for all non-union Town employees.
Personnel Committee: Seated left to right - Anne M. Davis, Chairman Lynn F. Markham
and Beatrice E. Stepton. Standing left to right - John L. Plank, Jr. and Paul F. Gelinas.

Conservation Commission
The Conservation Commission met twenty-two times in 1981 and held three public hearings. The decrease in
new construction was directly responsible for the low number of hearings under the Wetlands Protection Act, and
was much appreciated by the members of the Commission. This gave the Commission moretime to spend on planning
and the management of the large acreage of conservation land under its jurisdiction.
A large house and barn on the Laurelbank Farm Conservation Area on Pleasant Street was razed during the past
year. These buildings had fallen prey to vandals and had become an ugly eyesore in an area of developing rustic
beauty. The area was graded and seeded with bird-enticing grains and grasses. In a very few years, shrubs and trees
will obliterate any signs of former human habitation. All Townspeople are invited and encouraged to hike these
conservation areas. It is practically impossible to maintain propersigns because of constant vandalism on these pub-
lic properties. However, residents may call any of the Conservation Commission members for the location and uses
of these areas. Hiking, snowshoeing and cross country skiing are encouraged. Automobiles, trucks and motorcycles
are not.
The Conservation Commission lost two of its most valued members during the past year when Russell Harvey and
David Richards both movedout of Lunenburg. Robert Leupold, a registered forester, and Glenn Stillman, a graduate
horticulturist, were named to fill these vacancies on the Commission. Their knowledge in their respective fields can
only be a plus for the Conservation Commission and the Town of Lunenburg.
As we peer into the last two decades of the twentieth century, the Conservation Commission asks its fellow
Townspeople for help and encouragement in its quest for the protection of Lunenburg's water supplies. It would be
a terrible shame to leave our children a parched Lunenburg.
Conservation Commission - left to right - George Thomas, Robert C. Leupold, Chairman Raymond G. Chapdelaine,
Nancy I. Sholl and Edward H. Hall, Jr. Not present when photo was taken were John C. Lahti and Glenn Stillman.

Council on Aging
During 1981 the "Meals on Wheels" program was upgraded as the Council switched on a full time basis to the
Montachusett Nutrition Program - highly nutritious meals prepared with the elderly in mind. There is a higher cost
involved and there have been price increases, but the Council, along with users, is unanimous in praising this new
venture. The supervision of the volunteers who deliver the meals is now the responsibility of Sandra Flanagan, with
Martha Molaghan exercising the overall guidance of this program.
Due to budgetary restraints, the "Transportation for the Elderly" had to be cut back from two days to one day
per week. During the hours of 9AM to 1PM on Thursdays, senior citizens of our Town may be picked up at their
homes and taken to doctor's offices, shopping centers, banks, etc. Einard Warpula and his staff do a truly outstand-
ing job of extending this kind of care and concern.
The Drop-In Center under the supervision of Marcella Peed and with the volunteer help of Friendly Seniors,
continues to attract more and more seniors. It is open each Wednesday from 10AM to 1PM and its setting is a large
room in the United Parish Building. A committee made up of representatives from the Council on Aging and the
Friendly Seniors, has begun a serious search for a permanent facility for the seniors.
The Ninth Annual Christmas Dinner was held on December 10th in Andrews Hall at the United Parish. The
excellent dinner was once again prepared by Marjorie Stillman and served by volunteer help largely from St. Boniface
Church. Music was presented by the Chamber Singers of the High School and their Director, Donald Adams, who
also led in the singing of Christmas carols. This event seems to become better with each passing year.
During 1981, Dorothy Paradis resigned from the Council and is greatly missed. The Board of Selectmen ap-
pointed Sandra Flanagan to fill this vacancy. The Council also welcomes Helen White as a member of the Council.
After years of effective and dedicated leadership, Martha Molaghan did not choose to remain as Chairman of the
Council on Aging, but did remain on as a member. John Sandberg was chosen to fill the position of Chairman.

Inspector of Plumbing & Gas Fitting
During 1981 the Inspector of Plumbing and Gas Fittings made a total of 185 plumbing and gas inspections in-
cluding 29 for new homes, 24 for remodeling, 8 for solarheat installations, 8 for commercial buildings, 56 gas permits
for various gas appliances and 7 for commercial buildings. If in the opinion of the Inspector of Plumbing and Gas
Fittings, the Codehas been violated, permits maybe cancelled. Permits shall be invalid if work is not started within
90 days of the date thereon, unless the holder of such permit can satisfactorily prove that failure to start within the
90 days period was beyond his control.
On June 3, 1981, the Board of Massachusetts State Examiners of Plumbers and Gas Fitters held a public hearing
to discuss and determine appropriate action to be taken on a number of proposed amendments, revisions and deletions
to the Massachusetts Fuel, Gas and Plumbing Codes. The Board made the following interpretations: (1) Registration
by a licensed plumber in a city or town is only required once a year. (2) A licensed plumber does not have to appear
personally in a city or town to make an application to o' tain a plumbing permit. He may make application by mail.
The Inspector shall, except in the case of emergencies, perform his duties and inspections during the normal
working hours of the day. The Inspector of Plumbing and Gas Fittings for the Town of Lunenburg will continue to be
committed to those essentials necessary to provide the best workmanship for the protection of health and safety for the
citizens of Lunenburg.
Inspector of Weights & Measures
The breakdown for this Department for the year ending December 31, 1981 is as follows: 35 gasoline pumps
were inspected, tested and sealed; 3 pumps required adjustments. The number of scales inspected, tested and sealed
was 38, and adjustments were required on 6 scales.
This Department has had no complaints from consumers of products dispensed by the retailers covered by this
Department's inspections. However, consumers should feel free to call the Inspector of Weights & Measures or notify
the Town Hall of any problem that might develop. The Inspector will check into the problem and will advise the com-
plaining party of the outcome of the reinspection.
Again, your Inspector wishes to thank all the business establishments for their cooperation and assistance in
helping this Office carry out the regulations as required by law.

Inspector of Wires
The year 1981 was the initial year of the office of Inspector of Wires. As of August 1, 1981, all wiring done
in the Town must be performed by a licensed electrician and accompanied by the proper permit and inspections.
Generally, all wiring must be inspected in its "rough" stage and then its "finish" stage. All wiring must conform to
the Massachusetts Electrical Code (1981 edition). Before the Inspector of Wires cansign the certificate of occupancy
for new residences, these inspections must be made and full compliance to the Code must be exhibited.
Inspection of electric work is not limited to new residences, however. All wiring must be shown to meet ap-
plicable portions of the Code through inspections by this office. Examples of wiring which require the Inspector's
attention include (but are not limited to): homes, commercial/industrial properties, additions, garages, bathrooms,
electric service changes, and most important swimming pools. The safety and welfare of the citizens make wiring
inspections imperative.
Since August of 1981, a total of 22 permits for electrical wiring have been issued, and this office performed
31 inspections. Wiring permits may be obtained at the Town Clerk's Office.

Dog Officer
As Dog Officer I have many people in-
form me that the citation program is not effective.
I would like to explain the background and how the
program works. In 1981 a new schedule of fines
was voted on at the Annual Town Meeting. The
schedule is as follows: 1st Offense - $15.00; 2nd
Offense - $35.00; and every subsequent offense is
$50.00. Citations were also issued for every dog
in Town found unlicensed after April 1st. A total
of 615 citations were issued in 1981, and made
63 court appearances in behalf of the Town on these
citations. After an initial citation is issued, the
owner has 21 days to pay or appeal. If neither is
done, the Dog Officer then files a complaint which
in turn brings the owner to court for judgement.
If the owner feels that he is not guilty, he may
plead his case. The Town and the owner are fairly
treated, and as Dog Officer can properly enforce
the by-laws of the Town. In my opinion, the po-
sition of Dog Officer would be very ineffective
without a citation system.
The year 1981 was a very bad one with re-
gards to abandoned and stray dogs with a total of
233 stray dogs found in our Town. I was able to
give away 207 of these stray dogs to proper homes
and for use as seeing eye dogs and hearing aide
dogs. I have been very fortunate in placing these
dogs and in working with area cities and towns.,
and I hope I will continue to receive the help I
need to avoid having to put loving and healthy
Kathleen M. Comeau, Dog Officer
animals to sleep. Unfortunately, I was forced to
destroy 26 dogs in 1981 due to vicious temperment or severe contagious illnesses. Also in 1981, I was forced to put
20 dogs on permanent restraining orders due to severe problems with these animals.
The situation in the school yards has greatly improved in my opinion and that of school officials. The safety of
our children is very important to me and I will continue to enforce the rules at all times. There will be no exceptions.
I responded to 73 callson dogs that were hit by cars and had to bury 51 of these animals. The remaining 22 dogs were
lucky to survive and were transported to the hospital. At this time I would like to urge all owners of dogs to watch
your animals closely and not allow them to run loose or left unattended. In 1981, 25 dogs were quarantined due to
dog bites. As in past years, I would like to remind all dog owners that if your becomes lost to please call my home
immediately to report same and be sure that your is wearing his dog tag so that may be able to return them home
promptly. Please be sure that your dog is licensed prior to April 1st in order to prevent having to pay a fine and also
to cut down the hours I must spend on unlicensed dogs.
I would like to thank all residents of Lunenburg for their help and cooperation. I would like to give special
thanks to our Town Clerk and her staff, the Police Chief and his entire department, the Fire Chief and Rescue Unit
who have come to my many times. Of course, a special thank you goes to the Board of Selectmen, Town Counsel,
Richard Eley and the Selectmen's Office for their help and advice.
I am available 24 hours aday and you may call me at my home at 345-4957, and if I am not at home a message
will be taken and I will respond as soon as possible. I hope that no matter how great or how small your problem may
be, I may help you in some way.

Montachusett Regional Vocational Technical School District
Nineteen hundred eighty-one was the first full year under Chapter 580 of the Acts of 1980, commonly referred
to as Proposition 2½. Montachusett Regional Vocational Technical School endured all the pangs and problems exper-
ienced by the cities, towns, and other school systems throughout the Commonwealth. The budget process which OC-
curred early in the year was a difficult one for the school. Programs of proven value were discontinued. Reductions
in staff meant the loss of members who had been with the school since its earliest days. Emotions ran high. Among
the programs lost were all of those in the post secondary department: practical nurse, medical laboratory technician,
medical assistant, dental assistant. The two-year dental laboratory technician program is being phased out. At the
high school level, the child development and commercial art programs were marked for elimination and are now being
phased out.
In tune with the times, Monty Tech showed its concerns about energy conservation and costs, and in January
installed an Energy Management System which operates under computer control, and from the first day of operation,
there has been a show of demonstrable savings to the District.
In June, the largest Senior Class in the history of the school graduated - two hundred twenty-three students
and thirty-three post secondary students. Six medical laboratory assistants and twenty-seven practical nurses grad-
vated in August, the last post secondary graduation ceremony. During the year, plans for expansion which had started
in 1980 were carried on with varying degrees of success. By the end of the year, two towns, Orange and Petersham,
had voted to join the District; Westminster was undecided, and Committee activity on the expansion plans continued
into the new year.
The Massachusetts Association of Vocational Administrators held its annual workshop in June under the aegis
of the Department of Occupational Education and Fitchburg State College. As usual, it was well attended and
highly successful. Early in the year, the District Committee had voted to purchase a computer as opposed to the
previous practice of renting. The machine arrived in November with additional items of equipment arriving in Dec-
ember. All parts have been humming busily since installation. A new elevator for the andicapped was installed in
December.
The School Committee appointed a new Superintendent-Director in July, and Mrs. Charlotte A. Scott assumed
the position in September. At the end of December, James B. D'Donnell of Fitchburg, Holly A. Booth and Henry P.
Ares of Gardner, left the District Committee. The new Committee members (elected in November) are Charles A.
Dunphy, Sr. of Gardner and Helga D. Epstein of Fitchburg.
Among the many donations made to the school during the year was a 1981 damaged GMC one-half ton pick-up
truck from Chapdelaine Truck Center, Inc. of Lunenburg. The truck was marked specifically for the vocational train-
ing of students in the automotive department. The donations were not all one-way. Students in several vocational
shops combined efforts under the supervision of their instructors and repaired a fire truck for the City of Fitchburg.
An oak witness stand, a replica of the original stand from the old Fitchburg Court House, was made by the students
in the Cabinetmaking Shop, and has been delivered into the main court room of the new quarters. Other students
made a deck and access ramp for a former Monty Tech student who had been permanently injured in an accident.
There were many other services performed for the cities and towns of the District, most of which represented valuable
savings to the taxpayers.
Each year, students and faculty join at Christmas time to contribute to a local charity. In 1981, Royalston
was selected as the recipient town, and the Fireman's Relief Fund and the Policeman's Relief Fund were designated
as the charities to receive the funds, The total amount raised in 1981 was $363.02.
It was not the best of years, but assuredly, it was not the worst of years. Listed below are the member cities
and towns and their representatives for 1981:

Extension Service
The following were some of the activities of Lunenburg's 4-H Clubs during 1981. The Knitting Club members
learned how to cast on stitches, knit, purl, increase, decrease and to bind off stitches. They made potholders,
scarves, and some members were working on sweaters. The members in the Clubwere Todd McNiff, Larissa Sanderson,
Jean Marie Price and Katherine Lizotte. The meetings were held every Thursday at the home of Mrs. Harvey Price.
The Cooking Club had a very good year. They made such things as cakes, cookies, ice cream, candy, pies,
breads of all kinds and jellies. The girls that were in the Club were Jackie Dumais, Colleen Flanagan, Sharon
Flanagan, Tina Koney, Lonni Larsen, Rose LeBlanc, Jean Marie Price and Cindy Sue Matesowicz. The meetings
were held every Wednesday at the home of Mrs. Harvey Price.
The Lunenburg Bridle Path 4-H Horse Club has added another project to their Club, the Horse Lovers Group,
whose members do not own horses. The area has so many youngsters who want to learn about horses and this may be
the only opportunity they would have to fulfill their dreams. Present members are Lonni Larsen, Carolyn McCarthy
and Kristen Ridley. Mrs. Marion Benson started a junior leadership club project in Town with such topics as "I De-
cide", helping teenagers learn to make decisions on their own. Also on the agenda was horse judging and members
learned how to judge a horse's conformation, breed type, way of going, and the oral reasons for your placing the
class.
Club leaders are: Dixie Beck, who is in her 8th year of volunteer leadership and will retire at the end of this
calendar 4-H year, Paula Cloherty and Pat Fisher. Club officers are: President & Junior Leader, Jo Lynn Beck;
Vice President & Junior Leader, Jennifer Roy; Secretary, Pamela Ouellette; Newsletter Helpers, Erin Cloherty and
Lonni Larsen. Nancy Womble is assisting leaders as needed. Mrs. Fisher is leading our barnchecking program which
consists of visiting each member's barn to check on stable management, safety, records, feeding programs, and to be
there in case anyone needs help of any kind. Club jackets, t-shirts and sweatshirts were purchased by members.
The Club's activities have consisted of roller skating parties, visits to other club meetings, an equine shot
clinic, where 75 horses and ponies were attended to by Veterinarian Francis J. Fitzgerald of the Fitchburg Animal
Hospital. The Club took trips to the University of Connecticut and University of Massachusetts for horse related
education clinics. February always brings our visual presentation potluck supper including public speaking with
visual aids which are judged and placed. Many members start at Town level and go on to the Area level. If placed
high enough at each level, they can compete at County and State levels. Ann Marie Forkey, Heather Roy and Joe
Forkey have made it all the way to the Eastern States Fair in the past.
Lunenburg 4-H Clubs helped the Grange set up for their annual fall fair. The Horse Club spent a day cleaning
the grounds at the North Leominster Rod & Gun Club, to whom we are grateful for letting US use our riding ring on
their property for mounted meetings and horse shows. May brought a schooling show for "EVERYONE" (the new div-
ision for "PARENTS" to ride and show with their own championship division). This gives the parents a chance to see
just how much concentration and perfection it takes our youngsters to do a good job in the show ring. This division
brought a lot of fun and laughter.
The activity the members enjoyed the most this year was the five day stay at the University of Massachusetts
for the State 4-H Conference in June. Members were able to
select classes to attend on several different topics, from canoe-
ing, kyacking, hiking, radio broadcasting to basic horseman-
ship, nuclear energy, swimming and much more. They lived
in the dorms, ate meals in the campus cafeteria, attended each
class on schedule, attended dances, costume parties, and some

Ad Hoc Recycling Committee
Ad Hoc Recycling Committee: left to right - David D. Blake, Chairman James Patacchiola
and Operations Manager George G. Munyon, Jr. Other members not shown.
On October 19, 1981, the Board of Selectmen announced the formation of the Ad Hoc Recycling Committee
for the new Sanitary Landfill and the following individuals were appointed as members: Charles H. B. Arning,
David D. Blake, Paul F. Bregoli, James Patacchiola, James Mann representing the Planning Board, and Nancy 1.
Sholl representing the Conservation Commission. Mr. Patacchiola was subsequently chosen as Chairman of the Ad
Hoc Recycling Committee.
The Ad Hoc Recycling Committee has been meeting on a regular basis, and has recommended to the Board of
Selectmen that the following materials be recycled: bulky waste (large items, such as ovens, refrigerators, etc.),
newspaper, aluminum cans, glass (clear, green and brown), and motor oil.
Beginning in the spring of 1982, Town residents will be asked to seperate these items and dispose of them at
the proper recycling area in the Sanitary Landfill. The success of this program depends on the support and coopera-
tion of Town residents.

Sewerage & Drainage Investigative Committee
In May of 1981, the Town accepted a Federal Grant of $52,600 and a State Grant of $10,500 to conduct a
Facilities Planning Study. The engineering firmof Dufresne-Henry, Inc. began its work for the Sewerage and Drain-
age Investigative Committee in June and is continuing its evaluation of cost effective alternatives to an extensive
sewer system as of the date of this report.
Our engineer has identified areas in the Town where sewage disposal has been a problem through interviews
of homeowners, review of health department records, visual observations and a review of soil surveys. The findings
of this part of the Study were presented at a public meeting on November 5, 1981. Recommendations of the engi-
neer and public comments were used to formulate areas of the Town where more detailed planning should concentrate.
The next phase of the Facilities Planning Study will be presented at a public meeting on February 23, 1982.
The Sewerage and Drainage Investigative Committee will display several preliminary designs for each area of the
Town where alternatives to conventional individual subsurface sewage disposal may be considered. Public comment
will again be solicited.
Monthly Committee meetings are posted and held at the Town Hall. In addition, fact sheets describing the
progress of the Study are available at the Ritter Memorial Library. We urge you to acquaint yourself with this im-
portant Study and attend our meetings.
The Sewerage and Drainage Investigative Committee is made up of the following membership: Chairman
Bernard M. Freeman, C. David Hobson, Mary L. Padula, Chester J. Pliska and James T. Van Buren.

SCHOOL REPORT FOR 1981
===PAGE 40===
IN MEMORY OF -
MAURICE P. BILLINGS
1902 - 1981
On July 4, 1981, Maurice P. Billings, former
Lunenburg Superintendent of Schools, died suddenly
while visiting his son in Richland, Washington.
He also leaves a daughter and several grand-
children. He was married to the late Anna (Ahlin)
Billings.
Mr. Billings was born in Southeast Harbor,
Mt. Desert Island, Maine, on November 2, 1902, the
son of Alton and Bessie (Dow) Billings.
He was a graduate of the University of Maine,
class of 1929 with a bachelor of arts degree and
earned his master of education degree at Fitch-
burg State College in 1948.
He taught in Westminster from 1926 to 1943
and in the Hingham school system from 1943 to
1949 when he was named Superintendent of the
Ashby-Townsend-Lunenburg school district (Union
#27). He served in that position until 1954 when
he became Superintendent of the Lunenburg school
system (the year Lunenburg withdrew from the
three-town union).
He was a resident of Townsend and served as town selectman from 1963 to 1969, He was a mem-
ber of the Golden Age Club, Charles W. Moore Lodge of Masons, Fitchburg, the Townsend Historical
Society and the Amanda Dwight Entertainment Committee.
His successor, Ralph W. Brown, had this to say about Mr. Billings in the 1962 Town Report:
Under the leadership of Mr. Billings, Lunenburg had indeed developed its own school system.
Beautiful and functional physical plants had been constructed; a strong teaching corps, imbued
with enthusiasm for learning; capable and efficient clerical, custodial and lunch personnel had
been recruited so that a spirited type of educational program was already established. For these
reasons, one does not replace a man such as Mr. Billings, he can only hope to take his place.
His personal warmth of character, his keen sense of humor, combined with his dedication and
seriousness of purpose, are qualities I have been privileged to know. Consequently, we all shared
a feeling of loss to Lunenburg as Mr. Billings began his well-earned retirement on August 16,
1962.
On the occasion of his retirement, the parents, citizens, teachers and students of the Town
of Lunenburg presented the following CITATION to MAURICE P. BILLINGS:
In grateful recognition of your tireless and unselfish devotion to your responsibilities,
as the Superintendent of our schools
for the countless hours of faithful service to
the citizens and especially the youth of the Town of Lunenburg. Your decisions rested on
the criterion "best for the child". We express our deep appreciation for your conserva-
tive caution and "Yankee Tradesmanship"
ability in negotiating for large and small
purchases of pencils or pins, chalk or chowder, books or bricks, and maps or mats. You
have always displayed the attributes of a wise counselor, a true friend, a gentleman, and
one with a deep devotion to democratic practice and principle.
GIVEN AT LUNENBURG, MASSACHUSETTS, THIS THIRD DAY OF JUNE, NINETEEN HUNDRED AND SIXTY-TWO.

No-School Signals - 1981-82
The decision to close schools is made by the Superintendent of Schools after consulting with
the Highway Superintendent and the Police Department to determine the condition of the roads.
The practice is to close schools only in case of extreme weather conditions or unforeseen
emergency; therefore, when schools are in session on stormy days, parents are urged to exercise
their personal judgment as to the wisdom of sending their children to school.
No-School Signals for 1981-82
TWO SETS OF TWO (2) BLASTS ON THE HORN AT THE CENTRAL FIRE STATION
INDICATES "NO SCHOOL" AT THE FOLLOWING TIMES FOR THE GROUPS INDICATED -
6:30 AM
Grades 6 through 12
7:15 AM
Grades Kindergarten through 5
10:00 AM
PM Session of Kindergarten
Announcements will be carried on the regular broadcasts of WEIM and WFGL in Fitchburg WLMS in
Leominster; as well as WSRS, WTAG and WAAF in Worcester; and WBZ, WHDH and WROR in Boston, as
promptly as the decision is made.
The cancelling of school sessions requires the completion of numerous separate telephone calls
as rapidly as possible. The crowding of telephone circuits can mean that the signal center or bus
contractor might not be notified in time. Parents can help by not allowing their children to make
unneccessary calls. Cooperation and understanding by all will be very much appreciated.
Middle School students enjoying Mechanical Drawing
with student teacher, Mr. Askins

Annual Report of Superintendent of Schools
It is a privilege to submit my Fourth
Annual Report as Superintendent of Schools in
Lunenburg. This report covers the year ending
December 31, 1981.
As you read the reports of the other
administrators you will receive a more
detailed accounting of the events that have
taken place in the schools. The purpose of
this report is to summarize the total aspects
of the Lunenburg Public Schools.
THE STUDENTS & STAFF:
I have always been most impressed by the
quality demonstrated by the staff and students
in Lunenburg. The results of their efforts
continues to produce a well-educated student
and a committed staff member. Our teachers,
custodians, secretaries, cafeteria personnel,
and administrators provide each student with
the supportive help that is necessary tomain-
tain a quality school system. The dedication
of all of the individuals is reflected in the
results of the students' work, whether that
be in the area of academic accompl ishments,
plays, musical productions, athletics or
WILLIAM C. ALLARD, Ed. D.
daily attendance to their work. I must indi-
Superintendent of Schools
cate in this report what takes place in Lun-
enburg is in many instances far and beyond
what one would find in other school systems.
We are fortunate that we possess such talented students and staff to make our everyday efforts
worthwhile. I compliment and thank the parents of this community for providing such a supportive
home environment that is SO necessary for a quality educational system.
THE PAST YEAR:
This past year produced for us one of the most difficult years that public education has
ever faced in Massachusetts. As a result of the passage of Proposition 2½, we were forced to
reduce overall total expenditures for our schools from $4,138,975. to $3,506,166. or a total
reduction of 15.3% over the previous year. In reducing our budget by $632,809. one must remember
that we had to buy less in goods and commodities at an inflation price of 12%. In order to do
this, we were required to reduce our staff by some thirty professional people and in addition,
reduce our supportive staff (secretaries, custodial, cafeteria) by thirty people.
To accomplish such a reduction in finances, we were required to do the following:
1. Eliminate entirely some positions li. e., Assistant Superintendent of
Schools, Director of Reading E Language Arts, full-time Director of
Special Education, etc.)
2. Increase class sizes.
3. Eliminate custodial and secretarial positions.
4. Decrease athletic programs.
- 43
===PAGE 46===
5. Reduce cafeteria staff.
6. Eliminate teaching aides.
7. Eliminate all department heads and supervisors.
8. Eliminate all advisors to student body.
9. Eliminate late bussing.
10. Eliminate middle school after-school program.
11. Eliminate all subsidies to programs such as driver education,
cafeterias, and Title 1 teachers.
12. Eliminate all capital outlay.
It does not take one long in reading the preceding list to realize that in one year we were
forced to make many adjustments that affected our school system drastically. Always a problem
in planning to budget six to eight months before one is using it is the areas that start to show
up that one did not predicate. We presently are experiencing such difficulties and are respond-
ing to these as quickly and as thoroughly as possible.
In presenting the budget that I have outlined to you, I must indicate the very difficult
year that it was for your school committee. They, probably better than anyone else, had to make
the final decisions about expenses and the dismissal of a number of excellent staff members.
All of you should know that the school committee had to expend a great deal of time beyond what
is normally required to run a school system. Greater indepth study of the budget, hearings that
were required for dismissals and overall highly charged emotions in very difficult tasks were
required by the members of the school committee. Unlike other municipal bodies, the school
committee cannot raise its fees to offset a reduction in its finances. I would urge the citizens
in Lunenburg to understand and appreciate the efforts expended by its school committee in this
very difficult year.
THREE NEW PRINCIPALS:
As if the decisions necessary to carry out the implementation of Proposition 2½ on a school
budget were not enough, the school committee and myself embarked on the process to hire three
new principals. Mr. Carey and Mr. Barney had made their decision to retire and Mr. Ralston
resigned to take a new position in industry. At each level, a sub-committee of the school
committee and myself were appointed to screen the applicants and to interview candidates. In
all, over forty (40) applicants were interviewed by the sub-committees and a total of nine (9)
candidates were interviewed by the total school committee. As a result of these efforts, the
following individuals were appointed:
DR. A. RAY PETERSON Principal of Lunenburg High School
MS. CAROL FOLEY Principal of Turkey Hill Middle School
MRS. CONCETTA VERGE Principal of Passios Elementary School
and Lunenburg Primary School
I am sure that the citizens of Lunenburg will experience immediately the impact of these
three very capable and industrious educational leaders. They all have a great deal of work
ahead of them and I know that they will have the same support from the citizens, staff and stu-
dents that I have received since I have been here. The ability to attract such highly qualified
individuals to work here is a direct result of both the quality of the school system and a
highly professional school committee. They deserve and should demand the respect and support
that is necessary to carry out their assignments.
