In May, the school committee approved a policy change to move the entrance age for kinder-
garten-age students from December 1 to September 1. A student must now be five years of age by
September 1 to enter our kindergarten and six years old by September 1 to enter our first grade.
This change delayed formal schooling for approximately twenty-eight to thirty students who will be
entering this coming September. Although our enrollment decreased by one hundred twenty-nine (129)
students, our loss in personnel resulted in substantial increases in our class sizes.
We also introduced a number of changes that we have never had to implement in the past. These
changes were the result of the losses we suffered under Proposition 2½.
1. Introduction of user's Fees for the athletic program.
Each athlete must pay $10.
2. Introduction of fees for building use.
3. Place Adult Education on a self-sustaining basis.
4. Place Driver Education on a self-sustaining basis.
5. Place the Food Service Program on a self-sustaining basis.
6. Rely on booster clubs, Music Aiders and fund-raising events to financially
support our programs to a greater degree than in the past.
We appreciate the help that these organizations and groups have given to our programs for
without their contributions, we would have found it necessary to eliminate a portion or a total
program. We must also acknowledge a large cadre of volunteers that Mrs. Cheesman has produced to
work in our libraries. These volunteers have made it possible for us to keep our school libraries
available to our students. All in all, this has been a year to implement and be as creative as
possible with limited resources and staff. I would be remiss if I did not acknowledge the coopera- I
tion and willingness of all involved to come forth and demonstrate their talents or resources.
am sure everyone does this for the benefit of our students. They deserve and should demand the
best that we can provide for them. The end result of these efforts will be demonstrated by them
as productive citizens in our society.
THE FUTURE:
The future for the Lunenburg Public Schools is as difficult to write about as predicting the
rate of interest for borrowing funds over the next five years. I will, however, indicate that we
do have some future goals which will have some impact on our school system.
1. A complete analysis of our curriculum will take place in order to
insure that we are performing an integrated, well articulated K-12
curriculum in all areas.
2. Continued maintenance of our facilities in spite of decreased
financial resources.
45
===PAGE 48===
3. A complete and well documented study of the possibility of regionali-
zation.
4. A careful study of our enrollment projections and the impact that they
might have relative to curriculum and facilities.
5. Continued efforts to produce a budget that meets the needs of the
system to provide quality education to every individual entrusted to
our educational care.
6. Continuing efforts to keep our citizens aware of our concerns,
problems and successes.
7. Continuing efforts to seek additional funds in a time of declining
resources.
8. Increased efforts to maintain and encourage a highly qualified staff.
All of these goals will require the efforts of this administration and the citizens of Lunen-
burg to put forth its greatest efforts. I know that the citizens that I have worked with over four
years are prepared and willing to expend the energy and financial support that is necessary.
CONCLUSION:
In concluding this report, I would pay special thanks and appreciation to all the members of
the School Committee. Spending countless hours reviewing materials to establish budgets, policy,
the hiring and appointment of staff, the sincere effort to find out what is best for students and
finally having the courage to make decisions is most appreciated by this community. I thank each
member for this contribution and support. To my fellow administrators who have the responsibility
to establish a comfortable environment conducive to learning on a daily basis for our staff and
students, I thank them for their daily efforts and for their support. I wish to thank the staff
who on a daily basis, respond to the call of "teacher". Their efforts and professionalism make
the work that we do truly a rewarding experience. Special thanks go to my very capable executive
secretary, "Ginny" Welch, who succeeds every day to make my work and the work of the school system
function exceedingly well because she has both the talent and love for this school system that far
surpasses her job title. To Bob Okerman, who on a daily basis, never ceases to amaze me with his
dedication and knowledge. To the secretaries, cafeteria workers and custodians who provide the
smooth running of our business affairs, the daily "bread" of our school system, and who provide us
with clean and safe buildings, I thank them for their dedication and willingness to provide us
with their skills. Finally, to the townspeople, who continue to respect and support to the best
of their ability a school system that they have come to understand and desire to maintain. We
will not disappoint you with our efforts or our commitment.

Report of High School Principal
A. RAY PETERSON, Ed. D.
PHILIP M. CROUSE
Principal
Assistant Principal
It is a pleasure to present my first report as principal of Lunenburg High School. On July 1,
1981, my predecessor, Richard J. Carey, retired after thirty-five years of distinguished service
in the Lunenburg Public Schools.
In several ways, this is a time of major transition for this high school, which was establish-
ed in 1894-95. - With the rest of the nation, we are experiencing enrollment decline:
High School Enrollment
1970
-
844
1980
I
660
1981
I
630
We are also experiencing a sharp reduction in resources available to educate our students be-
cause of the passage in Lunenburg and statewide of the limitation on property taxes known as
Proposition 2½. At the High School we lost 20% of our teaching staff in the single year (FY-82)
cut of Proposition 2½. In addition, we suffered an operational/materials budget cut of 50% from
FY-81 to FY-82. We have lost one of three guidance counselors. and no longer have a staff
assistant in administration for attendance. In support services, our custodial staff was reduced
approximately 30%, and our clerical staff 40%. Besides these basic staff reductions, we no longer
have funds to support the middle management of the professional staff, the department heads and
head teachers who coordinated curriculum and instructional logistics. We no longer have a full-
- 47 -
===PAGE 50===
time librarian. In co-curricular activities, we have lost ten assistant athletic coaching posi-
tions, and have had to drop four underclass teams. We no longer have budgeted stipend payments
for teachers serving as student activity advisors in school yearbook, newspaper, math team, etc.
Varsity field hockey team in action in 1981.
These cuts have resulted in severe stress throughout the curriculum, the building, and
among the staff. We are operating with larger classes, fewer materials, less maintenance, and
have lost some student activities and support services. If we suffer any additional staff and/or
budget cuts, we will be forced to wipe out specific curriculum and co-curriculum offerings which
are crucial to maintenance of a balanced program for all students.
In these times of declining enrollment and declining resources, it is essential that the
community be aware of the standards of quality traditional at Lunenburg High School. In 1981, for
example, 60% of our eligible students took the College Board Scholastic Aptitude Tests (SAT), and
scored as follows:
National
Lunenburg
mean score
mean score
verbal
424
445
mathematics
466
495
This occurs in a high school in which, in the fall season alone, 30% (180 plus) of our stu-
dents participate fully in athletics and band.
The opportunities for quality experiences in our curriculum continue to develop. For example,
we have doubled our enrollment in computer literacy programs, which are now available to all stu-
dents during the high school years. We have also increased by 50% our numbers of students in work
study/work release experience in the community. The number of student enrollments in instrumental
and choral music program is up 36%, with more students continuing to audition and compete for
places in regional and state festivals.
- 48 -
===PAGE 51===
Our students continue to demonstrate high levels of creativity, organizational ability, and
responsibility in all activities. This fall, without class advisors for the first time, class
officers and student council organized and executed Homecoming float building, parade, and dance
with great success. In the winter term, students, on their own, organized for the first time in
several years a chess team which is successfully competing interscholastically
Planning for the years immediately ahead, our intention is to maintain and improve the flexi-
bility and "intellectual mobility" which has been the hallmark of the Lunenburg High School pro-
grams. A superior high school continually moves students across and up the academic structure.
We want out students to "stretch" into courses or activities never previously attempted. And in
those courses or activities our success is measured by the student's movement upward. to higher
achievement levels. We will continue to encourage ninth grade students to take strong general
education programs, and to take electives in disciplines new to the student throughout the four
years. We will also look to moving students upward in ability levels in courses as often as
possible. Few high schools have been as successful as Lunenburg High School in this effort over
the years, and we intend to continue this leadership. The community should understand that the
success of the high school, teachers and support staff, is a result of their high level of commit-
ment to fine quality, professional work. We look forward to expanding the cooperative educational
effort with the citizens of Lunenburg.

Report of Middle School Principal
CAROL E. FOLEY
RICHARD J. GARVEY
Principal
Administrative Assistant to Principal
The 1981-82 school year has been one of change and adaptation. In spite of financial reduc-
tions due to Proposition 2½, the middle school has thrived. Staff members and parents have
worked to meet the challenge of providing educational services to students with fewer resources
available. This was a year for determining educational priorities within the school and then
deciding how to provide for them.
MIDDLE SCHOOL LIBRARY:
In the area of library services, we opened our library with a staff of parent volunteers and
one remaining librarian, Mrs. Miriam Cheesman, who served all three schools. Mrs. Cheesman
trained the volunteers, worked out the schedule and helped them begin service. The teachers were
very much involved in helping this endeavor succeed. Teachers and volunteers helped one another
in working out scheduling of library time and in monitoring students. Without our volunteers,
the middle school faced the possibility of not having the library open until well after school
opened on September 1, 1981 and then only one or two days per week. We are most grateful to our
volunteers and most earnestly hope they will stay with us. Our library is open every day, all
day for student use, teacher use, and for special functions.
- 50
===PAGE 53===
The parent volunteer assisting
students in the library.
FUNDRAISING:
In order to save our after-school activity program, field trips and assembly program, it was
necessary to become seriously involved in fundraising. We did, and there is much to show for it.
Three seventh grade teachers--Harris Karis, Tom Starr and Philip Chalifoux--organized and ran a
magazine sale the first week of November 1981. Over $12,000. worth of subscriptions were sold,
40% of which returned to the middle school, approximately $4800. Without these funds, we would
have been unable to schedule our after-school activities, our assemblies, and field trips. We
were able to purchase a Sony Beta Max for instructional purposes with a portion of this money
gained from the sale.
The gains of this sale were not
only financial. Student morale was
very high during this period. In
addition to the major goal of saving
programs for the middle school, the
students worked for daily and overall
prizes for subscriptions sold.
Everyday during our lunch periods,
students were awarded prizes and
bonuses. Everyone received a prize,
even for only one subscription sold.
Two students sold over $400 worth of
subscriptions each. Out of our stu-
dent enrollment of 455, 267 partici-
pated in this program, making it an
outstanding success.

January. Programs of both schools were explained in detail. It is a high priority of the
counseling department to place students in programs which will be challenging and will focus each
student on a clear goal for the future. Student and parent desires for placement are given the
utmost consideration in the advisement process.
Each spring the counselors work closely with the teachers to place students in the seventh

For this age group, 11 - 13 years of age, a strong guidance program is an absolute necessity.
Both counselors offer a wide variety of services in addition to those mentioned already. Adoles-
cents need a great deal of attention and help in clarifying their values. In this middle school
our counselors address values clarification and programs in career education as well.
(2) HEALTH EDUCATION:
Again this is another area where adjustments had to be made to accommodate loss of resources
and staff. Health education is a curricular area which cannot be withdrawn from a viable middle
school program. The staff and students have adjusted to health education taught in cycles to the
sixth, seventh and eighth grades, each grade level receiving a third of the academic year. Bar-
bara Jezak, health teacher, is serving the whole system this year and teaches three periods per
week in the middle school. She has been emphasizing alcohol and drug prevention, as well as in-
formation pertaining to cigarettes. Sex education and nutrition have been included in the program
at all three levels. Films, filmstrips and pamphlets have been used to motivate students. Dale
Diamantopoulos, our physical education teacher, has assisted in the area of smoking prevention by
presenting a program on lung cancer for the seventh and eighth grades. Middle school teachers
place a high priority on educating their students concerning choices involving improvement in
health attitudes.

THE ANNUAL CHRISTMAS FAIR:
Another year and another outstanding success involving nearly every sixth grade student and
nearly every middle school teacher--this describes the Christmas Fair. Jeff O'Neill, a sixth
grade teacher, organizes this fundraising project yearly with the support of parents, teachers
and students. Seven hundred dollars was raised for the Lunenburg Community Council this December.
The event is the highlight of the year and this year involved the senior citizens of the town as
well. Contributions and participation are widespread and greatly add to the spirit of the build-
ing.
In total, the year was one of growth and adaptation and problem-solving. We had to look at
our purposes very carefully and assess what was most necessary to accomplish. We believe we have
made correct choices and that our planning for the next few years will bring about changes that
are beneficial for middle school children.
My first year as principal has been an. exciting learning experience. I thank Dick Garvey
and Agnes Withington, especially for their patience and continuing help in the building. Dick
Garvey assumed the responsibility for additional teaching assignment, bringing the total up to
three classes as well as his many administrative duties. Agnes Withington ran the office all
alone this year and kept me constantly informed and aware of events daily. The total staff has
done a very fine job of pulling together new and veteran members in a difficult year.

CONCETTA A. VERGE
ROBERT FINNERON

ENROLLMENT INFORMATION:
Current combined enrollment of the Primary School (kindergarten and grade one) and the Passios
Elementary School (grades 2-5) totals 697 students. This is a decline of approximately fifty stu-
dents from school year 1980-81.
A portion of the enrollment decline is the result of a new school committee ruling which man-
dates that all children entering kindergarten shall have reached age five by September 1 prior to
entering school that year. As a result of this policy we have a very small kindergarten popula-
tion for school year 1981-82, but will reap a large harvest of youngsters next fall (school year
1982-83).
The following represents the numerical configuration of student population at the elementary
level as of February 1, 1982:

===PAGE 59===
ELEMENTARY SCHOOL COPING WITH PROPOSITION 2½:
Although Proposition 2½ has had a devastating effect on numbers of staff at the elementary
level, Lunenburg educators are trying desperately to maintain the quality educational program for
which this town is famous.
Class size has reached a disproportionate teacher/pupil ratio. It is not uncommon for class-
room teachers to be dealing with an additional seven or eight students more per class than they
have been accustomed to in previous years.
Classroom methodology and materials have had to be altered to adequately provide for attention
to student needs. Maintaining a high quality elementary school on a "shoestring" with a "skeleton
crew" is no easy task. However, the community should be made aware that a very dedicated, very
professional group of educators are attempting to do just that by giving 150% of themselves. Need-
less to say, we have an exceptional yet exhausted staff.
The school committee and superintendent have been very responsive to emergency needs of the
students by providing additional part-time assistance as required by either state mandate or over-
crowded conditions.
Curriculum assessment has taken place and a curriculum development plan is being formulated.
There will be heavy teacher-input in the development of the curriculum, Curriculum development
will not be an easy task given our fiscal constraints. However, the enthusiasm generated by the
staff and the willingness to become involved is universal.
CONTEST WINNERS:
construct a poster for the statewide Health Awareness contest, sponsored by the Massachusetts
Last April (1981) four students from Mrs. Ruth Hathaway's kindergarten worked together to
Medical Society and Auxiliary for grades K-6. Diana Alatalo, Patricia Blake, Andrew Schaff and
David Walchner drew a crayon picture entitled "On The Road To Good Health." They placed third in
awards ceremony at the Park Plaza Hotel in Boston, were presented with individual award certifi-
the Kindergarten division for the entire state! Such jubilation! They attended a fun-filled
cates, ribbons and a special T shirt. Their poster has been on display with other winning entries
at various places throughout the state--locally at Burbank, Leominster, Nashoba and Henry Heywood
Hospitals. Eventually it is hoped the poster will return to be housed in T. C. Passios Elementary
School.
ENERGY CONSERVATION:
For several years the Lunenburg schools have been concentrating on conserving energy. During
the summer of 1981 a number of improvements were made to the T. C. Passios Elementary building to
help cut utility and heating costs.
Ceilings were lowered under the skylights in all classrooms by the installation of insulated well
ceiling panels. This allowed for the removal of a length of heating pipe in each room, as as
the retention of room heat at the lower level.
The incandescent lights in the row under the skylights in each room were replaced with energy
efficient fluorescent lights. In addition, all the incandescent lights in fifteen of the twenty-
four classrooms were replaced at this time. The remainder will be replaced as funds permit.
The lobby of the Passios School was enclosed by the addition of a set of interior doors time at the the
corridor end. It was felt that this will save much of the heat that was being lost each
outer doors were opened. It is the goal of the School Department to make this building as energy-
efficient as possible.

Retirements
FLOYD F. BARNEY
September 1, 1947 June 30, 1981
(from Fitchburg Sentinel
Monday, May 11, 1981 issue)
After thirty-four years in the
Lunenburg School System, Floyd F.
Barney is retiring this June. In
1947, when then Superintendent of
Schools Charles Robinson asked recent
college graduate Floyd Barney if he
would help out and fill an empty teach-
ing position, the latter hesitated. He
was home visiting his parents and about
to accept a job in New Hampshire and
didn't think he wanted to teach. He
replied, "I'll fill in for awhile, but
I don't want to stay." Well, he did
stay and found he liked teaching and he
liked kids.
Barney grew up in Lunenburg. After high school, he went to Springfield College. World War
II interrupted his college career. While in the Army, stationed in Texas, he met his wife-to-be,
Gladys. They married and after he was discharged, he returned to college and was active in
sports, especially basketball. In 1947, he was named by Boston sportswriters as the "Best Play-
maker" in New England. That year he graduated and began teaching in Lunenburg.
Barney partially credits his career to being in the "right place at the right time." In 1958
he coached the boys' basketball team to the Wachusett League championship and in 1961, did the
same for the girls' basketball team. After teaching for many years, he became the assistant prin-
cipal and then the principal of the newly built Turkey Hill Middle School (1967).
He remembers the first day at the new school. "We moved into the Middle School in February
1968. It was a very orderly move. Everyone had saved shopping bags for weeks before and on that
day-it was a Monday-the kids carried their books in the bags to the new building. Then we had
a snowstorm you wouldn't believe. We sent the kids home by noon and stayed-closed all week." The
following Monday, school reopened. "It was a little hectic and about a half hour later (the snow)
came down in buckets." This time school was able to resume the next day.
He hasn't planned exactly what he'll be doing once he is retired. He enjoys gardening and
tends a big vegetable and flower garden. He expects that will keep him busy until October or
November. He used to train bird dogs and hunt, but hasn't done that for awhile because there
"just isn't time." The part of the hunt he liked was the dogs. Barney spoke with enthusiasm.
"I had more fun watching a dog in action. There is nothing more beautiful." His daughter, Beth,
needlepointed a picture of an English Pointer which now hangs on his office wall. This interest
started as a boy when he used to watch a professional dog trainer who lived in town, work with
his dogs.
Paperwork is one aspect of the job that he will not miss. Over the years as principal he
has seen the paperwork increase to great proportions. At times he has felt "locked in" and not
able to spend as much time dealing with people as he would like. "I will miss the people. I
have a great staff of teachers, secretaries, custodians, and cafeteria workers. I've enjoyed the
kids. The vast majority of kids are good kids. Ninety percent are hardworking."
Barney considers teaching one of the most demanding jobs a person can have. "If you build a
boat and it has a burr in it you can throw it away. A child with a burr in it-you must smooth
066." He thinks teaching now is more difficult than it used to be. Years ago, a child who was
not motivated "dropped out and could get a job." Now teachers have to cope with them and try to
motivate them. "When the bell rings you get rid of 25 kids, but 25 more come in. They are all
different with different personalities. 1 have great respect for teachers.'
- 60
===PAGE 63===
RICHARD J. CAREY
September 1, 1946 June 30, 1981
(from Fitchburg Sentinel,
Wednesday, April 1, 1981)
Thirty-five years ago a young man from Natick
applied for a teaching position in a town he'd
never heard of--Lunenburg. Richard J. Carey got
the job. He began as a teacher and the varsity
baseball and basketball coach, later moved on to
the assistant principalship and nineteen years ago
was appointed high school principal.
Carey comes from a family of teachers with
two brothers also in education. Once his eldest
brother was his English teacher. When report cards
were handed out, the other students gathered to
see what mark he received. "A 'B'," they said,
"You're lucky your brother is the teacher." "I
went home and told my brother never to give me a
'B' again. Don't flunk me, but give me a 'C', I
said."
He is sure his own children suffered some
from having gone through Lunenburg schools with
their father as principal. It was a rule that they
were not to talk to me about school, except academic areas, not about other students. I'm sure
they had problems that I didn't hear about.
Before coming to town, Carey served in World War II, flying heavy bomber missions over
Germany. "I was a member of a nine-man crew and we became like a family. We still keep in touch."
They celebrated their 35th Reunion at the Pocono Mountains last summer. It was such a success
that they plan to get together every two or three years instead of the five to ten they had been
seeing each other.
An active man, Carey frequently rises to point out something that illustrates what he is
saving: an aerial photograph from about 1948 which hangs on his wall showing what is now the
Primary School and the Historical Society Building, then the entire Lunenburg School System--the
way it was when he arrived. The plaque that tells when the present high school was built, and
the shelves below the window where the high school yearbooks are kept. He uses them to look up
students whose name he may have forgotten, but on seeing their pictures he remembers them.
"I like the kids. I take a half-dozen walks around the halls daily to see what is going on.
I also do cafeteria duty every day. I don't have to, but it's a good way to have conversations
and keep in touch with the kids. In that low-key atmosphere, it is easier to seek out someone
with a problem and talk to them than to call them into the o office where a student gets on their
guard. I don't think the kids are afraid of me. Not that I can't give a class the devil when it
is needed. But I also make sure they know why a penalty is invoked."
He met his wife, Priscilla, in his first year of teaching in Lunenburg where she was also a
teacher. They married and raised five children. "I never moved on to another school because my
family was settled and 1 liked living here."
He speaks with obvious pride about his family. "Priscilla is a first grade teacher in Leo-
minster. She took ten years off to care for our family." The children are all college graduates.
"Now that they are all out of college, 1 can retire. Mary Pat, his oldest is a professional
dancer in New York City. Richard, Jr., works in Lowell as a computer programmer. Rosamond ("I
am the only one who calls her that-to everyone else she is Posey") works with Mary Padula at the
Town Hall. Christopher lives in California and works on construction. The youngest, Priscilla,
is a chemical engineer.
"I don't know how it will be when 1 retire (this June). I'll sleep an extra half hour in
the morning, he laughs. "No, retirement is not mandatory, but 1 want to retire while I can still
enjoy myself. 1 think I have gotten along well with the kids and the personnel. I will miss
being with the people every day and all the comings and goings. I'm going to miss the action."
- 61 -
===PAGE 64===
RICHARD J. ALLAIN
September 1957 June 1981
A 1954 graduate of Fitchburg State College receiving his bachelor of science degree, Dick
entered the armed services. Upon his discharge in 1956, he was hired as a teacher of grade six
in the Lunenburg Elementary School. He received his master of education degree from the same
college on August 11, 1961. With the opening of school in September 1967, Dick was transferred
to Lunenburg High School to teach science, including electronics, to replace a teacher who had
resigned. Over his years of tenure he had taken courses at Fitchburg State College, Framingham
State College, Lowell Technical Institute, and attended various in-service courses accumulating
forty-five credits beyond his master's degree. Due to Proposition 2½, Dick chose to take advan-
tage of an "early retirement" and after twenty-five years of outstanding service to the Lunenburg
Public Schools, he retired effective June 30, 1981.
ELIZABETH R. LAKSO
February 1960 June 1981
Betty received her bachelor of science degree from Hyannis State College in 1942. Prior to
coming to Lunenburg on February 29, 1960 as a teacher of grade two, she taught grade two in South-
bridge for one year; grade three in Needham for two years, and grades two & three for two years
in Reading. After twenty-one and one-half years of dedicated service, Betty also took advantage
of an "early retirement" and retired effective June 30, 1981.
GLORIA C. SPADARO
January 1959 - August 1964; September 1968 - June 1981
Gloria was a 1953 graduate of Fitchburg State College receiving a bachelor of science degree.
She began in Lunenburg on January 5, 1959 as an elementary physical education teacher (a new
position for the school system). On August 31, 1964 she resigned to accept a similar position in
Natick. When the new middle school was opened in September 1968, she returned to Lunenburg as
girls physical education teacher. Due to Proposition 2½, Gloria retired effective June 30, 1981,
after completing over seventeen years of faithful service.
GLADYS H. VAN HORNE
October 1966 - June 1981
Gladys was a 1950 graduate of Salem State Teachers College receiving a bachelor of science
degree. She was a substitute teacher in the Lunenburg Public Schools at the elementary level
during 1943-44 and 1964 to 1966. On October 3, 1966 she began as part-time remedial reading
teacher under P. L. 89-10, ESEA, Title I and effective September 1, 1969 became full-time with
duties as both remedial reading and math teacher. At the time of her retirement on June 30,
1981, she was serving as remedial math teacher under the Title I grant, completing fifteen years
of devoted service to the students in her care.
IRWIN W. KRESSER, JR.
May 1971 June 1981
"Ike" began as our Maintenance Mechanic on May 18, 1971, a newly created position. He has
served the school system well and has left his mark on us with the craftsmanship of his efforts
in the schools and offices. On June 23, 1981, "Ike" chose to begin a well-earned retirement. We
all will (and have) missed his presence and talent.
LAURIER P. LETARTE
April 1971 May 1981
Laurie began as a custodian at the Lunenburg Jr.-Sr. High School on April 5, 1971. On July
5, 1977 he transferred to the Turkey Hill Middle School as senior custodian. He applied for and
received the appointment to transfer to the Elementary Annex on July 6, 1978, He retired from
this position on May 20, 1981 after ten years of faithful service.

ALPHONSE L. THIBAULT
February 1976 - May 1981
Al began as a custodian on February 18, 1976 under the CETA program. On February 18, 1977,
he became a permanent employee of the Lunenburg Public Schools. On April 17, 1978, he was
appointed as senior custodian at the Turkey Hill Middle School. He retired on May 15, 1981. We
will all miss his annual staff spaghetti dinner. Even though retired, he is still serving as the
school Santa Claus.

CLASS SONG:
"IMAGINE" by John Lennon
Imagine there's no heaven, it's easy if you try,
no hell below us, above us only sky.
Imagine all the people living for today.
Imagine there's no countries, it isn't hard to do,
nothing to kill or die for, and no religion too.
Imagine all the people living life in peace,
you may say I'm a dreamer, but I'm not the
only one.
I hope someday you'll join us, and the world will
be as one.
Imagine no possessions, I wonder if you can,
no need for greed or hunger, a brotherhood of man.
Imagine all the people sharing all the world.
You may say I'm a dreamer, but I'm not the only one.
I hope someday you'll join us, and the world will live as one.

Town Clerk, Tax Collector, Treasurer
TOWN CLERK - The Town Clerk's position carries with it
the responsibility of maintaining a list of public ways
in the Town. Since 1728, when Lunenburg was incorpor-
ated, the town has voted to accept, alter or discontinue
the various roads in Town. Many of the original accept-
ances contained wording such as "thence across on the
dam till it comes two rod south of a large pine stump
on the south side of the pond " (March 31, 1798) It
has been a problem determining where such roads are 10-
cated today. Through the years, cities and towns have
more or less bluffed their way through this dilemma.
In recent years, the courts have begun to make commun-
ities more accountable in this regard and cities and
towns are having to produce proof of "facts" based on
assumptions. The Town Clerk has been fortunate in being
able to engage Robert Ebersole, a third year law student
willing to work for minimum wages, who is also a member
of the Planning Board and an active and interested mem-
ber of the town's Historical Society, to carry out the
necessary research. With this information, the town
will now be able to back up the decision of whether a
MARY L. PADULA
road is public or not. This determination is also vital
for the administration of Lunenburg's affairs on a day
to day basis. Many departments in town need to know if a way is public. The Highway Department
needs to know because the Massachusetts General Laws require that public ways "shall be kept in re-
pair at the expense of the town. " The Selectmen need to know for they regulate the use of streets.
The police need to know for prosecuting traffic or open bottle bylaw violations. The Planning Board
needs to know for sub-division purposes. The Building Inspector needs to know for zoning conside-
rations. Town Counsel needs to know so that the town can avoid huge damage awards for personal
or property damages. Additionally, this research will result in a valuable historical reference
source. Many layouts of roads not previously in the town's possession have been located. These
should prevent those situations which in the past have required new engineering layouts for highway,
sewer or water line installations. This alone should save the town thousands of dollars over the
years.
Another Town Clerk's record which has particular significance in guiding Lunenburg's future is
the record of births. The 1982 Annual Town Meeting will be asked to create a Regional School Dist-
rict Study Committee. One of the assumptions prompting this study relates to school enrollment
projections which are in part based on birth statistics. It is important that both past and present
local birth trends be accurately considered in any such projections, as well as up to the minute
local, state and national demographic trends. The following graph demonstrates that erratic birth
trends during the last decade could produce equally erratic projections.

ABOUT THE COVER PAGE - For years municipal funds have gathered dust (but not interest) in checking
accounts at local banks. The State required Cash Management Achievement Chart on the preceding page
demonstrates that this is no longer the case. Cities and Towns throughout the Commonwealth are now
typically over 80% invested. There are bankers and others who will argue that this shift in municipal
investment policy is damaging local economies by redirecting several billions of dollars that could
otherwise be used for real and personal property mortgages. Although this arguement is politically
powerful and indeed has merit, the trend toward tighter investment control of municipal monies is
not expected to reverse as long as cities and towns like Lunenburg, (where the approximately $200,000
in annual investment earnings offset $2.35 on the Fiscal Year 1982 tax rate) can continue to generate
hundreds of thousands of additional municipal dollars.
In spite of the constraints of Proposition 2½, Lunenburg is extremely healthy. We ended Fiscal
Year '81 with an unrestricted fund balance of $520,155 and no uncollected Real Estate taxes. Our
Fiscal Year '82 tax bills were mailed on time and Real Estate taxes were received on or before the
usual November 1 deadline. We have not had to borrow in anticipation of revenue since 1973. We were
able to weather the first year of Proposition 2½ without any appreciable reduction in services and,
even with a substantial reduction in our teaching staff, our present pupil/teacher-supervisor ratio
is under seventeen. We do not at the present time anticipate further reductions in staff in Fiscal
Year '83 and could inflate current operating budgets an estimated seven percent and still absorb a
$100,000 decrease in State Aid. Current prognostications by State Legislators notwithstanding, we
are confident that State Aid will not be cut in 1982 for that type of thing simply does not happen
in the State of Massachusetts during an Election Year. The following computation supports our con-
fidence regarding Lunenburg's financial well-being in Fiscal Year 1983:
Special purpose non-recurring FY '82 appropriation,

It should not be necessary for Lunenburg to override any of the provisions of Proposition 2½ in
Fiscal Year '83.
Due to Proposition 2½, the total amount to be raised by taxation in FY '82 was $729,410 less
than in the previous year and the average Lunenburg taxpayer realized a savings on his tax bill of
$350 (see chart page 102). The circle graphs on page 98 demonstrate that whereas in FY '81, 62% of
the town's revenue was derived from property taxes, in FY '82 the burden on the local property tax
was reduced to 54%. The reduction of the motor vehicle excise rate from $66 per $1,000 to $25 per
$1,000 valuation, also mandated by Proposition 2½, accounted for further substantial decreases in local
revenue derived directly from the taxpayer.
Proposition 2½ has forced cities and towns throughout the Commonwealth to re-think policies
affecting the conduct of government affairs in general, and hiring and personnel practices in part-
icular. Local officials have been surprised to learn the magnitude of hidden payroll costs, and in
many communities municipal employees are being replaced by contract labor on an attrition basis. For
example, seven maintenance personnel with combined yearly earnings of $75,281 additionally inflate
the total town expenses by $25,163 as follows:
Town's share (75%) Blue Cross/Blue Shield & Insurance

ance premiums due to poor experience, or future retirement costs.

In the face of shameful public pension abuses, communities are finally starting to tighten their
hiring policies including yearly physical exams by an impartial community designated physician.
Whereas one (1) out of one thousand (1,000) persons retire with a disability pension under the Social
Security System, one (1) out of four (4) public employees receive disability retirement. This mis-
carriage of justice also contributes to the size of this State's UNFUNDED PENSION LIABILITY, where
the average yearly increase in public pension benefit payments during the last five years equalled
28%. An article will be inserted in the FY '82 Annual Meeting Warrant requesting $40,000 to commence
to fund Lunenburg's future share of the Unfunded Pension Liability. If an equal amount is set aside
each year for the next ten years and invested at an average of 12%=, the fund would then total
$788,000. Commencing with the 11th year, we could theoretically each year appropriate the interest
on the aforementioned amount which at 12% would amount to $95,000 and which added to the then accus-
tomed $40,000 appropriation would represent a $135,000 yearly payment towards our inevitable, size-
able, otherwise unfunded pension liability.

===PAGE 125===
Vital Statistics
Following are those records of Births, Marriages and Deaths that either occurred in our Town or records which were forwarded
to this Town from other municipalities in the State. Lunenburg residents are urged to notify the Town Clerk if in-State records have
not been received and recorded. If a Birth, Marriageor Death occurred outside the State, the same may be recorded in the Lunen-
burg Town Clerk's office if certified records are presented. In some cases, the Town Clerk's records are supplemented by records of
burials which appear on Page 12 of this report. In order for these records to also be included in the official Vital Statistics records,
the family of the deceased should present a certified copy of the death certificate to the Town Clerk, In accordance with recent
changes in the General Laws, marriages which occur here but relate to licenses which were obtained elsewhere in the State are no
longer included in our offical Town Records.

Excerpts from Annual Town Meeting Minutes
April 4, 1981
SPECIAL FISCAL YEAR 1981 WARRANT:
ARTICLE 1. To see if the Town will vote to raise by taxotion and appropriate, or appropriate from available funds, a sum of
money to revalue all personal and real property in the Town, the Board of Assessors to be authorized to enter into contractual agree-
ments with an appraisal firm or firms to complete said revaluation, or act anything thereon. (Approved by the Finance Committee.)
VOTED THAT THE TOWN APPROPRIATE FROM SURPLUS REVENUE THE SUM OF $117,000 TO REVALUE ALL PERSONAL AND
REAL PROPERTY IN THE TOWN AND THAT THE BOARD OF ASSESSORS BE AUTHORIZED TO ENTER INTO A CONTRACT WITH
A PROFESSIONAL APPRAISAL FIRM TO COMPLETE SAID REVALUATION.
ARTICLE 2. To see if the Town will vote to raise by taxation and appropriate, or appropriate from available funds, a sum of
money to update property values, and authorize the Assessors to engagesuch assistance or expertise as is necessary to fulfill statutory
obligations related thereto, or act anything thereon. (Disapproved by the Finance Committee.) MOTION DEFEATED.
FISCAL YEAR 1982 ANNUAL TOWN MEETING WARRANT:
ARTICLE 1. To choose by ballot the following officers: Selectman for three years; Assessor for three years; Auditor for one
year; two School Committee Members for three years; Park Commissioner for three years; Commissioner of Trust Funds for three years;
Cemetery Commissioner for three years; two Library Trustees for three years; Planning Board Member for five years; Planning Board
Member for two years; Housing Authority Member for five years. RESULTS OF ELECTION FOLLOWS:

ARTICLE 2. To see if the Town will vote to accept the regular annual written reports of the Town Officers and Committees,
or act anything thereon. VOTED UNANIMOUSLY.
ARTICLE 3. To see if the Town will vote to amend Article XV of the Town's By-laws entitled "Salary Administration Plan of
the Town of Lunenburg" by deleting the Classification and Salary Schedule voted on April 5, 1980, to be effective the beginning
of the payroll week including July 1, 1980, and substituting therefor the Classification and Salary Schedule proposed by the Per-
sonnel Committee and appearing in the report of the Finance Committee, such action to be effective the beginning of the payroll
week including July 1, 1981, or act anything thereon. VOTED.
ARTICLE 4. To see if the Town will vote to amend Article XV of the Town's By-laws entitled "Salary Administration Plan of
the Town of Lunenburg" by: (a) deleting from Section 19. SICK LEAVE, the first two paragraphs, and inserting in place thereof the
following two new paragraphs: "All persons permanently employed, whether full time or regularly part time (minimum twenty (20)
hours per week) and who have completed six (6) months probationary period by March 1 of any year, shall be entitled to sick leave
of ten (10) days, to be credited on March 1. New employees having completed the six (6) months probationary period shall be cred-
ited as of the end of said six (6) months, with sick leave in the amount of one (1) day for each full month intervening between the six
months anniversary date and the next March 1, up to a maximum of ten (10) days. Use of such leave must be occasioned by sickness
or injury or by exposure to contagious disease, and shall not be authorized or permitted if such sickness or injury or disability was
self-imposed or resulted from the use of alcohol or drugs. Unused sick leaveshall be cumulative, and shall be carried forward on March
1 of each year, to be added to the sick leave credited as of March 1.", and by (b) deleting in its entirety Section 19A. PERSONAL
DAYS, and inserting in place thereof the following new section: Section 19A. PERSONAL LEAVE "All persons permanently employed,
whether full time or regularly part time (minimum twenty (20) hours per week) and who have completed six (6) months probationary
period by July 1 of any year, shall be entitled to personal leave of two (2) days, to become available on July 1 for use during the
ensuing fiscal year. New employees having completed the six (6) months probationary period shall be credited as of the end of said
six (6) months, with one (1) personal day for use during the period between said six (6) months anniversary and the end of the then
current fiscal year; provided, however, that the said six (6) months probationary period must have been completed by May 1 of that
year. Personal Leave shall be scheduled with the approval of the Department Head. Personal Leave shall not be cumulative.' or
act anything thereon. VOTED UNANIMOUSLY.
ARTICLE 5. To see if the Town will vote to raise by taxation and appropriate, or appropriate from available funds, all sums
of money necessary to defray the charges and expenses of the Town for Fiscal Year 1982 and to fix the salaries and compensation of
all elected officers of the Town and any other items included in the budget of the Finance Committee, or act anything thereon.
VOTED UNANIMOUSLY ON MOTION OF THE FINANCE COMMITTEE THAT THE BUDGET BE TAKEN UP AT THIS TIME AND
THAT THE TOWN APPROPRIATE THOSE SPECIFIC SUMS OF MONEY RECOMMENDED IN THE LAST COLUMN OF THE BUDGET
APPEARING ON PAGES 2, 3 AND 4 OF THE FINANCE COMMITTEE REPORT WITH THE FOLLOWING EXCEPTIONS: THAT ITEM
3, SELECTMEN'S SALARY BE INCREASED BY $450 TO $3000, A REDUCTION TO BE PROPOSED UNDER ITEM44BEING AN OFF-
SET TO SUCH INCREASE; THAT ITEM 9, ASSESSORS' SALARY BE INCREASED BY $450 TO $3000, A REDUCTION TO BE PRO-
POSED UNDER ITEM 44 BEING AN OFFSET TO SUCH INCREASE; THAT ITEM 44 BE REDUCED BY $1,080 TO 286; THAT ITEM
45 BE REDUCED BY $2,086 TO $21, 169; THAT ITEM 63 BE INCREASED BY $489 TO $89,489, A PORTION OF THE REDUCTION
UNDER ITEM 45 BEING AN OFFSET TO SUCH INCREASE; AND THAT TO MEET SUCH APPROPRIATIONS $198,927 BE TRANS-
FERRED FROM SURPLUS REVENUE; $500 BE TRANSFERRED FROM AMOUNT VOTED UNDER ARTICLE 29 OF THE 1976 ANNUAL
TOWN MEETING WARRANT TO REPAIR THE TOWN HALL SIDEWALKS; $44.00 TO BE TRANSFERRED FROM AMOUNT VOTED
UNDER ARTICLE 7 OF THE 1977 ANNUAL TOWN MEETING WARRANT TO UPDATE THE TOWN'S BY-LAWS; $1, 665 BE TRANS-
FERRED FROM AMOUNT VOTED AT THE 1979 ANNUAL TOWN MEETING UNDER ARTICLE 32 FOR TWO POLICE CRUISERS AND
$5,214.50 FROM AMOUNT VOTED UNDER ARTICLE 7 TO ACQUIRE LAND FOR THE MRDA AND $5,600 FROM AMOUNT VOTED
UNDER ARTICLE 37 FOR TENNIS COURTS; $375.66 BE TRANSFERRED FROM AMOUNT VOTED AT THE 1980 ANNUAL TOWN
MEETING UNDER ARTICLE 36 FOR OILING ROADS, NORTH CEMETERY AND $510 FROM AMOUNT VOTED UNDER ARTICLE 27
TO DEMOLISH THE LAURELBANK HOUSE; $151.53 BE APPROPRIATED FROM INTEREST EARNED ON THE ANTI-RECESSION
- 128 -

ARTICLE 6. To see if the Town will vote to amend Section VI of its Protective By-law by adding thereto procedural and de-
sign requirements for driveways in order to provide moximum protection to the public through orderly control of traffic moving onto
and from a street, to provide uniformity in the design and construction of driveways and to provide adequate drainage where required,
by adding a new sub-section to be known as sub-section D, to read as presented to the Town Meeting in the text of the handout
supplied, or act anything thereon. VOTED THAT THE TOWN AMEND ITS PROTECTIVE BY-LAW BY ADDING AFTER SECTION
VI-C, A NEW SECTION TO BE KNOWN AS SECTION VI-D, AS FOLLOWS:
D. Driveways and Entrances
1. Purpose
a. To provide maximum protection to the public through the orderly control of traffic moving onto and from
a street.
b. To provide a uniform practice in the design and layout of driveways and entrances.
C. To provide for adequate drainage where required.
2. Procedure
Prior to any construction of a driveway, an owner shall make written application for approval to the Building
Inspector acting through the Selectmen. Before approval is granted, the application shall be referred to the
Highway Superintendent and if necessary the Conservation Commission.
- 130 -
===PAGE 133===
3. Design Requirements
a. Entrances shall be located to the best advantage with regard to street alignment, profile, sight distance
and safety conditions as determined by the Highway Superintendent.
b. Entrances to a given tract of land must be made from an accepted or approved Town way or from those
ways in existence prior to October 2, 1951.
C. Driveway grades and locations shall be such to provide for an access for vehicles, including fire and police.
d. Not more than two dwellings may maintain one common entrance and/or driveway.
e. Minimum Entrance Width: (measured from the edge of road surface to a point fifteen (15) feet onto the
applicont's property)
Minimum
Maximum
Residential
10'
16'
Commercial
One Way
11'
20'
Two Way
22'
30'
Surfacing requirements are not covered by this section.
f. Location
Wherever possible, entronces are to be set back 50 feet rmore from a street corner measured between the
nearest edge of the driveway and the cross road edge of pavement.
g. Entrances off State Highways shall conform to Massachusetts Department of Public Works Standards and
Regulations.
h. Any adjacent disturbed areas shall be returned to a useable state.
i. The Town reserves the right to inspect the proposed site before, during and after construction.

any note or notes as maybe given for a period of less than one year in accordance with the General Laws, Chapter 44, Section 17,
or act anything thereon. VOTED UNANIMOUSLY.
ARTICLE 38. To see if the Town will vote to approve an amendment to the Cemetery Rules and Regulations, such amendment
having been adopted by the Cemetery Commissioners on February 18, 1981, or act anything thereon. VOTED UNANIMOUSLY
THAT THE TOWN APPROVE THE AMENDMENT TO THE CEMETERY RULES AND REGULATIONS ADOPTED BY THE CEMETERY
COMMISSIONERS ON FEBRUARY 18, 1981, AS FOLLOWS: INCREASE COST OF BURIALS DURING MONTHS OF APRIL 1 THRU
DECEMBER 1 TO $80.00.
ARTICLE 39. To see if the Town will vote to accept the several sums of money for Perpetual Care of various cemetery lots,

Excerpts from Special Town Meeting Minutes
September 17, 1981
ARTICLE 1. To see if the Town will vote to hear a report by the Board of Selectmen on the conduct of affairs under the
terms of Proposition 2½¹₂ and the recently enacted State Budget for FY82, or act anything thereon. VOTED.
ARTICLE 2. To see if the Town will vote to appropriate the sum of $250,000, or some other sum or sums, for the construction
and/or reconstruction of the Sewage Disposal Systems serving the Turkey Hill Middle School and the Lunenburg High School and also
to provide Sewage Disposal Systems facilities for the Passios Elementary School and determine how the money shall be provided,
whether by taxation, transfer from available funds, borrowing or a combination of the foregoing and, if by borrowing, to authorize
the issue and sale of bonds or notes, or act anything thereon. (Approved by the Finance Committee.) MOTION DEFEATED.
ARTICLE 3. To see if the Town will vote to raise by taxation and appropriate, or appropriate from available funds, the sum
of $13,500, or some other sum or sums, to supplement the Fiscal Year 1982 budget, item number 49-Highway Labor appropriation
made at the Annual Town Meetingo April 4, 1981, or act anything thereon, VOTED UNANIMOUSLY TO PASS OVER ARTICLE 3.
ARTICLE 4. To see if the Town will vote to raise by taxation and appropriate, or appropriate from available funds, the sum
of $19,000, or some other sum or sums, to supplement the Fiscal Year 1982 budget, item number 42-Municipal Refuse Disposal ap-
propriation made at the Annual Town Meeting on April 4, 1981, or act anything thereon. (Disapproved by the Finance Committee.)
MOTION DEFEATED.
ARTICLE 5. To see if the Town will vote to raise by taxation and appropriate, or appropriate from available funds, the sum
of $8,500, or some other sum or sums, for the purchase of one new police cruiser, or act anything thereon. (Disapproved by the
Finance Committee.) VOTED THAT THE TOWN APPROPRIATE THE SUM OF $8,500 FOR THE PURCHASE OF ONE NEW POLICE
CRUISER AND THAT TO MEET SUCH APPROPRIATION, $6,866 BE TRANSFERRED FROM THE AMOUNT VOTED AT THE APRIL 4,
1981 ANNUAL TOWN MEETING UNDER ARTICLE 28 FOR THE PURCHASE OF A POLICE CRUISER, AND $1, 634 BE RAISED BY
TAXATION AND APPROPRIATED.
ARTICLE 6. To see if the Town will vote to raise by taxation and appropriate, or appropriate from available funds, the sum
of $6,000, or some other sum or sums, for the purchase of radio equipment for the Police Department, or act anything thereon. (Dis-
approved by the Finance Committee.) MOTION DEFEATED.

sums of money to be set aside in the stabilization fund, in accordance with MGLA Chapter 40, Section 5B, or act anything thereon.
VOTED THAT THE TOWN RAISE BY TAXATION AND APPROPRIATE THE SUM OF $200,000 TO BE SET ASIDE IN THE STABILIZA-
TION FUND, IN ACCORDANCE WITH MGLA CHAPTER 40, SECTION 5B.
ARTICLE 12. To see if the Town will vote to accept the provisions of MGLA Chapter 90, Section 20A (concerning local en-
forcement of Parking Regulations) and appropriate the sum of $500, or some other sum or sums, for implementation of the above, or
act anything thereon. (Approved by the Finance Committee.) VOTED THAT THE TOWN ACCEPT THE PROVISIONS OF MGLA
CHAPTER 90, SECTION 20A, AS MOST RECENTLY AMENDED, TO BECOME EFFECTIVE ON JANUARY 1, 1982, AND TO RAISE
BY TAXATION AND APPROPRIATE THE SUM OF $500 FOR IMPLEMENTATION OF THE ABOVE.
ARTICLE 13. To see if the Town will vote to accept the following amendment to the agreement with Montachusett Regional
Vocational Technical School District. The Agreement with respect to the establishment of a vocational, regional school district
entered into pursuant to Chapter 468 of the Acts of 1964, as amended by Chapter 543 of the Acts of 1965, by and between the cities
of Fitchburg and Gardner, and the Towns of Ashby, Barre, Harvard, Hubbardston, Lunenburg, Royalston, Sterling and Winchendon,
the said municipalities being the constituent members of Montachusett Regional Vocational Technical School District, is hereby
amended in accordance with the procedures forth therein, to include in the said Regional Vocational Technical School District,
as members thereof, the Towns of Orange, Petersham and Westminster; any, either or all of the said towns. Section 1, Paragraph (C)
is amended by adding thereto the following: At the annual town meeting first following the acceptance of the proposed amendment
to the agreement establishing the Montachusett Regional Vocational Technical School District by the member cities and towns thereof
and upon acceptance by the town or towns seeking admission to the District under the terms of the agreement as so amended, the
said town or towns of Orange, Petersham and Westminster shall each elect one member to serve on the Committee for a term of one
year, and at the annual town elections in 1982, and in every fourth year thereafter, shall each elect one member to serve for a term
of four years. Section 1, Paragraph (E) Organization shall be amended as follows: The term of office of any member elected by a
member city shall commence on January 1 following the election of such member and the term of office of any member elected by a
member town shall commence on the first regular meeting following the election of such member. Promptly upon the election and
qualification of the member, the Committee shall on the last regular meeting in June organize and choose by ballot a chairman and
vice chairman from its own membership The term of office of a member elected by the towns of Orange, Petersham and West-
minster at the first annual town meeting following acceptance of the amended agreement, shall commence at the first Committee
meeting following such acceptance. Each such town of Orange, Petersham and Westminster shall elect one member to serve for a
term of four years at the annual town meeting of 1982, and at the annual town elections in every fourth year thereafter. Section
IV, Paragraph (D) Apportionment of Capital Costs is hereby amended by inserting therein the following subparagraph: (D) (1) Upon
the admission of any city or town to the District, as a member thereof, as provided in Section IX hereof. Such city/town shall bear
its proportionate share of the capital costs as provided for in Section IV D hereof. In addition, such city or town shall pay into the
District such sum as may be determined as its proportionate share of capital costs incurred and borne by the District for each year
since the establishment of the District to the date of the admission of such city or town as a constituent member of the District. Pay-
ment of such assessment may be made as determined by the Montachusett Regional Vocational Technical School Committee at the
date of such admission, or act anything thereon. VOTED UNANIMOUSLY.
